# March — TODO List

**Last updated:** 2026-07-29 (refinement-guarantee sweep, one branch: a glob import no longer withdraws the measure aliases on sight — one `import Process` in `stdlib/system.march` had disabled the `List.length` alias for EVERY March program, invisibly, since a skip exits 0 just like a proof; `cap no_panic` retires every fact about a rebound name — `if d == 0 do 0 else (let d = 0; 10 / d) end` passed `--check` then panicked; a withdrawn alias explains itself under `cap verified` instead of blaming z3; `cap no_panic` discharges a divisor refinement before rejecting it; a refinement on an `impl` method parameter obliges callers or binds nobody; both capability walks — and desugar's `strip_entry_self_qual` — exhaustive over `A.decl`; the CI obligation ratchet gained a proof FLOOR; also merged in: the optimizer's purity oracle no longer misjudges a monomorphized builtin call as pure (2026-07-28); see below and Done.)

This file tracks everything that still needs to get done. Organized by priority and category. Check `specs/progress.md` for what's already done.

## Refinement legibility follow-ups (OPEN, from the 2026-07-28 `--refine-report`/measure-alias/`cap verified` work)
- **No `@[trusted]` escape hatch.** `cap verified` has no way to accept a single
  obligation the checker cannot discharge; the only outs are `assert` or dropping
  the capability. Until this exists, `cap verified` is not usable at scale.
- **A refinement in an `interface`'s OWN method signature is unenforced.**
  `fn run : a -> {Int | _ > 0} -> Int` written in the interface obliges no call
  site. Nothing assumes it either, so this is a missing check rather than an
  unsound one — but it silently does nothing, which is the failure mode this
  area keeps producing. The supported spelling is a refinement on the `impl`
  method's parameter (adopted when the method name is unambiguous).
- **Postconditions are not in the ledger.** `check_post` neither records nor
  escalates, so `--refine-report` undercounts by every undischarged *return*
  refinement and `cap verified` silently permits one. Still open as of
  2026-07-29 and stated in both `specs/lang/refinement-types.md` and
  `docs/refinement-types.md`.
- **The measure-alias gates are unit-global.** One genuine competing binding
  anywhere in the compilation unit — the prepended stdlib, or any
  `MARCH_LIB_PATH` dependency the author never opened — withdraws
  `List.length` / `String.byte_size` / `string_byte_length` as measure aliases
  for the entire program. Deciding whether a competitor could actually win at a
  given call site needs a resolver the pass does not have there, and the error
  directions are asymmetric (over-withdraw = silence, under-withdraw = a false
  positive), so the coarseness is deliberate — but it is a real coverage cost
  and the glob-import regression below shows how invisibly it can bite.
- **`collect_direct_names` (`lib/desugar/desugar.ml`) still ends in a wildcard**,
  covering only `DFn` and `DLet`. It is the fifth walk of this family and the one
  that was not made exhaustive; it decides which self-qualified spellings
  `strip_entry_self_qual` rewrites.
- **Impl-method contract adoption ignores `use`-imported impl methods** when
  judging whether a method name is ambiguous: `adoptable_impl_methods` counts
  only the compilation unit's own `DFn`/`DImpl` declarations.
- **`alias-withdrawn` attribution does not follow a laundered guard.**
  `let n = List.length(ys)` then `if n > 0` falls back to the general
  `solver-undecided` message even when the withdrawal really was the cause.
- **A qualified spelling INSIDE a predicate enforces nothing, silently.**
  `{List(Int) | List.length(_) > 0}` parses and typechecks, and enforces
  nothing at all: refinement predicates are not run through desugar, so
  `List.length` stays an `EField` chain rather than the dotted `EVar` the alias
  keys on, and the obligation is skipped. Pre-existing, not introduced here —
  but it is the one case this branch leaves invisible, which is exactly the
  failure mode `--refine-report` exists to surface. At minimum the pass should
  WARN on an unreflectable qualified call in a predicate; the fuller fix is to
  desugar predicate expressions the way bodies are. (The supported spelling is
  the bare measure, `len(_) > 0`.)

## A refined `let` annotation is checked against its RHS (CLOSED 2026-07-30)

`let ys : {List(Int) | len(_) > 0} = []` used to report `1 proved, 0 violated,
0 skipped` and exit 0 even under `cap verified`: the annotation entered the
scope channel unconditionally, so `inner(ys)` was **falsely proved** off a
premise nothing had established. It was the one refined position in the
language that obliged nobody.

**Closed** by `check_let_annotation` (`lib/refinecheck/refine_check.ml`), which
reflects the binding as a synthesized one-parameter call — the annotation is the
precondition, the bound expression the sole argument — and routes it through
`check_call`, inheriting every resolver and the definite-failure stance. Two
details are load-bearing: `param_names` carries the LET NAME (not the
refinement's binder), which is what makes the `len(ys)` spelling resolve; and
the bound name is shadowed out of all three fact channels first, so an outer
value's fact can never be attributed to this binder.

An unproven annotation now grants **no fact**, rather than being
unverified-but-trusted: `let ys : {List(Int) | len(_) > 0} = zs` for an opaque
`zs` leaves the annotation skipped AND the downstream call skipped, instead of
proving the call off an assumption the binding failed to establish. A proof
resting on an unverified premise is precisely what this closes.

Side effect: the bound-name spelling at `Int` (`{Int | n > 0}` over `let n`),
previously not resolved at all and merely skipped, is now checked too. All three
spellings (`_`, a declared binder, the bound name) behave alike. Bracketed by
`accept/t130_refine_let_annotation_checked_and_composes` /
`reject/t131_refine_let_annotation_false`; +7 refinecheck tests (358 → 365).
Stdlib and corpus contain zero refined `let` annotations, so nothing in-tree
changed behaviour.

## Refinement contract composition follow-ups (OPEN, from the 2026-07-29 call-boundary composition work)

- **A caller-established runtime GUARD is a different mechanism** from a
  caller's declared contract. `if List.length(ys) > 0 do head(ys)` already
  worked (the `len` alias, 2026-07-28) and is untouched. Which shape you have
  decides which machinery you get.
- **Postconditions are still outside the ledger** — `check_post` composes no
  list or ADT measure through a return refinement, and files no obligation.
- **The measure-alias gates are still unit-global.**
- **There is still no `@[trusted]`.**

**Follow-ups.**
- ~~**A tag refinement still does not forward.** `{Option(Int) | is_Some(_)}`
  passed on to a callee with the identical contract stays skipped
  (`1 proved, 1 skipped`, the proof being the outer literal). Tag facts are
  established at the call site by a constructor literal or a `match` narrowing,
  not carried by a binding, so this is the one refined form composition does not
  cover.~~
  **CLOSED 2026-07-29** — `load_scope_tester_facts` (`lib/refinecheck/refine_check.ml`,
  the tester analogue of `load_scope_measure_facts`, wired into `check_call`'s
  `resolve_tester` before it reflects the actual) loads the caller's own tag
  promise over the same `Const x` datatype term the goal side builds, so
  `fn outer(p : {Option(Int) | is_Some(_)}) do inner(p) end` now reports
  `2 proved, 0 violated, 0 skipped`. All three spellings of the refined value
  compose (`_`, a declared binder, the parameter's own name). Deliberately
  narrow: the loader fires only when the caller promises the SAME constructor
  the goal tests — a caller promising `is_None(_)` into a callee wanting
  `is_Some(_)` loads nothing and stays skipped (assuming it verbatim would also
  be sound and would report the call, but it is a wider claim than "the caller
  already promised the goal"). Rebinding (`let p = None`) or a `match`-arm
  binder of the same name retires the fact. Bracketed by `accept/t129` /
  `reject/t130`; +6 refinecheck tests (352 → 358).
- **No local `let` carries a value forward into a later goal, for ANY type.**
  `let u = 5` then `take_pos(u)` against `{Int | _ > 0}` is skipped, and the
  `List` analogue behaves identically. Pre-existing and general (it is also why
  rebinding a refined parameter leaves the call skipped rather than reported);
  the workarounds are to pass the value directly or restate the fact with
  `assert`.

## Open follow-up: test-file registry drift

A test `.march` file can silently drop out of the build (nothing checks that
every `test/stdlib/test_*.march` is registered in `test_stdlib_march.ml` — this
is how `test/stdlib/test_json.march` went dead for a while, wired into no
runner). A directory-vs-registry assertion would catch it.

## Merge-loss round 2 — 14 commits on `docs/core-march-types-skeleton` never reached `main` (2026-07-24)

**A second, distinct merge loss from the same branch as the 2026-07-18 audit
(which was recorded as COMPLETE).** `git merge-base --is-ancestor <sha> HEAD`
says NO for all of these; `git log --oneline main..e283d9b9` counts 14.

### Audit results (2026-07-24) — the branch holds 31 non-docs commits, not 13

Method: patch re-application is useless after months of drift (169/275 came
back "ambiguous"), so each commit was scored by **content presence** — what
fraction of its substantive added code lines exist anywhere in `lib/ bin/
runtime/ stdlib/ forge/ lsp/ js/` today. Calibrated against three known
outcomes: `e283d9b9` (restored this session) → 100%, `c30161d7` and
`4b4c70b3` (restored by the 2026-07-18 audit) → 100% / 90%. Script kept at
`scratchpad/content_audit.sh`.

**A low score means "this implementation is absent", NOT "the bug is live"** —
several were superseded by a different fix that IS in `main` (e.g. `5a648f9c`
to_string-via-Show scores 0% but was superseded by `6d2eed85`, and to_string
on containers verifiably works). Every claim below is a *behavioural* probe,
not a line count.

- [ ] **CLASS BUG behind the deque case, still open: lazy vs eager stdlib
  loading changes REPRESENTATION decisions.** Eager-loading one module is a
  point fix; any other lazily-loaded module exporting a generic function over
  a niche-able type (`Option`/`Result`) has the same latent
  boxed-vs-niche decode mismatch, and it fails SILENTLY (wrong value, no
  diagnostic). Either make lazy loading typecheck enough to resolve caller
  binders, or make mono refuse to emit a call it could not specialize
  instead of falling back to the generic body. Audit the rest of
  `stdlib_file_list`'s complement for the same shape.

**Probed and NOT reproducing** (superseded, or the probe doesn't hit it):
`d2d0a3a3` (`examples/stats_basic.march` interp==compiled parity ok),
`895ebfee` (`bench/dataframe_bench.march` runs clean), `5a648f9c`
(to_string-on-container works, superseded by `6d2eed85`), `c30161d7`,
`4b4c70b3`, `b7140673`, `778d399c`, `a7f96dad` (all score PRESENT).

- [ ] **Still unprobed, ranked by risk** — each needs a behavioural test
  before any verdict: `f2b67001` (niche-erased Option FBIP reuse → RC
  underflow), `f2729935` / `6f047e02` (Perceus RC field-type resolution),
  `6dd1968c` (B18 TCO release-timing), `4a58e992` (Task.race/any/cancel —
  possibly superseded by the 2026-07-18 cancel-token work), `03498340`
  (node_discovery stack overflow; the torn-stdout half is superseded by
  `e73644aa`), `c430e330` (reject overlapping impls), `b267a436` (derived
  structural Show), `b84ae429` (monomorphism restriction), `fcfd78ba` +
  `3c8826a0` + `4ab998ea` (the three-stage uniform apply-fn ABI flip — these
  are a *sequence*; restoring one without the others is likely worse than
  restoring none), `d5562dfc`, `1a547481`, `21f4fbb2`, `8f624a50`,
  `7868160e`, `65eefa53`.

### Why the safety nets missed all of this

- [ ] **The differential oracle cannot see these crashes.**
  `bench/iolist_template.march` is `SKIPPED`; `string_pipeline` and
  `deque_ops` are `INTERP_TIMEOUT`. The sweep only compares programs where
  the tree-walking interpreter produced ground truth inside its 10s budget,
  so for every compute-heavy benchmark — exactly the ones most likely to
  exercise RC, FBIP and TCO — a compiled SIGBUS, hang, or wrong answer is
  invisible. The sweep reported **0 divergences** on the same day three bench
  programs were broken compiled. Give these an expected-stdout anchor and
  compare the compiled run against it directly, with no interpreter leg.
- [ ] **`march --compile` should write its intermediate `.ll` next to the
  OUTPUT, not next to the source.** Writing beside the source pollutes the
  tree and makes compiling from a read-only directory impossible. Found by
  the bench gate failing inside dune's sandbox.
- [ ] **Process gap, not just a backlog.** Two separate audits of this branch
  have now each declared completion while leaving real fixes behind. Before
  the next one, decide on a mechanical check (e.g. a CI job asserting no
  `fix(`/`feat(` commit on a merged branch is missing from `main`) rather than
  a third manual pass.

## Large-operand interpolation is quadratic again — needs `string_concat_n` (OPEN, 2026-07-27)

`fceea07b` removed the `string_join` cons-list path, so every interpolation now
desugars to a `++` chain folded into three-way `string_concat3` calls. That is
the right call for the common case — measured here with SHORT (8-byte)
operands, the concat3 chain beats `string_join` at *every* size tested, with no
crossover up to 20 operands:

| operands | chain | join |
|---|---|---|
| 2  | 30ms  | 59ms  |
| 5  | 77ms  | 108ms |
| 8  | 121ms | 157ms |
| 20 | 302ms | 350ms |

But `string_concat3` still re-copies the accumulated prefix at every step, so
with LARGE operands the single remaining shape is quadratic, and the join path
that used to cover that band is gone:

| operands | chain (4KB operands) | join (4KB operands) |
|---|---|---|
| 4  | 16ms   | 13ms |
| 8  | 87ms   | 23ms |
| 16 | 356ms  | 49ms |
| 32 | 1236ms | 97ms |

Restoring a count-based threshold would not fix this: the parser knows operand
COUNT but not operand SIZE, and these two tables disagree about the right
choice at the same count. The fix that removes the tradeoff entirely is a
length-summing **`string_concat_n`** — sum all parts once, allocate once, copy
once — which would dominate both shapes at every size with no cons cells.
Blocked on the constraint that forced the fixed-arity `string_concat3` fold in
the first place: March builtin signatures are `Mono (TArrow ...)` and cannot be
variadic, so this needs either a variadic builtin mechanism or a
spread-into-array calling shape.


## Performance regressions vs the 2026-03-24 benchmark table (OPEN, 2026-07-24)

Measured with `RUNS=10 bash bench/run_benchmarks.sh` on the same machine
class, with OCaml/Rust as controls (both within noise of their 2026-03-24
figures, so the host is not the variable). See `bench/RESULTS.md`.

### ROOT-CAUSED AND FIXED 2026-07-24 — the per-call check was a TLS resolver call

**`66371f3b` (2026-03-25) "feat(sched): Phase 4 — reduction counting in
compiled code" landed the day AFTER the 2026-03-24 benchmark table was
recorded.** Every compiled function entry now emits a load / decrement /
store against the `@march_tls_reductions` global plus a conditional branch to
`@march_yield_from_compiled` (`Llvm_ctx.emit_reduction_check`, gated only on
`ctx.repl`). So the published table is a pre-preemption baseline and every
number since carries this cost. **This is the price of a deliberate feature,
not a regression to hunt** — but it was never re-baselined, so it has been
sitting in the table as an unexplained 2.2x.

A/B measured by gating the emission behind a temporary env var, **with
`.march/cas/artifacts` cleared between every build** (see the warning below):

| benchmark | TLS check (before) | plain-flag check (now) | no check at all | 2026-03-24 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `fib(40)` | 640 ms | **465 ms** | 360 ms | 288 ms |
| `tree_transform` | 852 ms | **579 ms** | 540 ms | 513 ms |
| `list_ops` | 67 ms | **64 ms** | 80 ms* | 68 ms |
| `binary_trees` | 177 ms | **165 ms** | 150 ms | 265 ms |

\* the allocation-bound benchmarks are within noise of each other; the check
cost falls almost entirely on call-dense code.

Removing it lands `fib` and `tree_transform` essentially back on their
historical figures, which closes both open items. Allocation-bound
benchmarks are unaffected — the cost is per *call*, so it falls entirely on
call-dense recursive code.

Ruled out along the way, so nobody re-walks them: the tag/untag round-trip
through the `case` result slot (`shl`/`or`/`inttoptr` on store, conditional
`ashr`+`select` on load — present today, a bare `inttoptr` in 2026-03; in
isolation the tagged form is *marginally faster*), March's clang flags
(`-fno-strict-aliasing -fwrapv -msse4.2` — no effect), and scheduler thread
count (`MARCH_NUM_SCHEDULERS=1` changes nothing).

- [ ] **Remaining `fib` gap vs 2026-03-24 (~390 vs 288 ms): the per-iteration
  volatile check plus call frame.** ~100 ms over ~165M loop iterations
  (post-TRE) ≈ 2 cycles/iteration — the price of staying preemptible. Any
  further recovery means checking less often (e.g. unrolling the TRE loop or
  a check-every-N scheme), which re-opens the counter-cost question; not
  obviously worth it.
- [ ] **Re-baseline `bench/RESULTS.md` against a post-preemption reference**
  so the table stops implying a regression that is actually a feature cost.

> ⚠️ **Benchmark A/B methodology — the CAS artifact cache will lie to you.**
> The first run of this experiment concluded "the reduction check costs
> nothing", because compiled artifacts are content-hash cached in
> `<project>/.march/cas/artifacts/` and the key covers compiler flags but
> **not environment variables**. Both arms of the A/B were served the same
> cached binary. Always `rm -rf .march/cas/artifacts` between arms and
> confirm the two outputs have *different* hashes before believing a null
> result. (Same class as the existing `cas_flags` note for CLI flags.)
- **Not a regression:** `binary-trees(15)` improved (265.4 → 176.6 ms) and
  `list-ops(1M)` is exactly restored (67.6 → 67.3 ms).

## Compiler: `pfn` unreachable from a sibling in the same NESTED module (OPEN, 2026-07-24)

- [ ] **A public `fn` in a nested `mod` cannot call a `pfn` declared beside it
  in that same module** — reported as `Module `X` does not export `y``.
  Top-level modules are unaffected. Since the project convention is exactly
  one top-level `mod` per file, nested modules are the normal way to have
  several modules, so this breaks ordinary private-helper code. Minimal repro:

  ```march
  mod Outer do
    mod Crypto do
      fn encode(x : Int) : Int do scramble(x) end
      pfn scramble(x : Int) : Int do x * 31 end
    end
  end
  -- Module `Crypto` does not export `scramble`.
  ```

  Found by the differential oracle: `examples/modules.march` (whose Part 3 is
  a deliberate pub-vs-`pfn` demonstration) now fails to typecheck at all, so
  it reports `INTERP_FAIL` and reddens the sweep. Suspected interaction
  between the 0.2.0 "Module does not export" diagnostic change and the
  intra-module qualification pass (`specs/2026-06-23-desugar-intra-module-qualification.md`)
  rewriting the bare call to a qualified one before the export check runs.

## Quarantined tests — coverage that is currently DARK (inventory, 2026-07-24)

**Read this before assuming a green CI run means the corresponding behavior works.**
Five tests have been pulled out of `dune runtest` onto their own aliases. Four of them
are quarantined on **genuinely unresolved concurrency races, not on test bugs** — the
behavior each one pins is unverified on every commit, and a regression in that behavior
would not turn CI red. Quarantining was a containment decision (these hung or flaked CI,
in one case silently consuming the full 6h GitHub Actions job ceiling), explicitly not a
fix.

| Alias | Pinned behavior now unverified | Blocked on |
|---|---|---|
| `test/node_call_loopback_quarantined` | multi-node RPC over real TCP loopback | deadlock is FIXED, but local verification is blocked by shared-host port collisions: these tests bind FIXED ports (29850, ...) and this dev host runs several concurrent March sessions — found a 34-hour-old zombie `native_node_call_loopback` holding 29850 (causing "bind failed" + a stale-era server answering with "malformed hello"). Un-quarantine after either (a) a clean soak on an isolated host/CI, or (b) making the tests bind port 0 / a per-run port. |
| `test/node_discovery_quarantined` | SWIM node discovery / membership | same port-collision verification blocker |
| `test/rpc_auto_enroll_quarantined` | RPC auto-enrollment handshake | same (was quarantined preemptively; the deadlock it was quarantined against is now fixed) |
| `test/signal_term_suppress_quarantined` | a watched `SIGTERM` must NOT kill the process | `Signal.watch` deferred-dispatch race (entry below) |
| `forge/test/build_check_quarantined` | `forge build` end-to-end check | a CI/local discrepancy **plus** a real 3-way qualified-call constructor-resolution bug |

## Memory-ordering audit of the runtime's lock-free paths (2026-07-24, post-deadlock-fix)

After the `task_await` store-buffering bug, every atomic site in `runtime/` was
audited for the same class: a lock-free **store followed by a load of a
DIFFERENT atomic location** whose correctness argument assumes sequential
consistency (Dekker pairs), plus lifecycle check-then-act races. Method: full
inventory of `atomic_*` sites per file (scheduler 78, runtime 48, dispatch 31,
deque 15, http 17, message 6, reload/ffi 4), then per-protocol classification.

**CLEAN — verified sound:**
- `march_deque.h` — textbook Lê-et-al C11 Chase-Lev: `pop` carries the seq_cst
  fence between its bottom-store and top-load (the exact SB shape that bit
  `task_wait_done`), `steal` fences between its two loads, CASes seq_cst. Even
  documents the TSan-vs-standalone-fence subtlety.
- Global runq — mutex-protected push/pop; the lock-free empty fast path has
  bounded staleness (re-polled every dispatch iteration) and its "a queued
  proc still counts as live" shutdown argument checks out against the
  `g_live_procs` decrement site (only on PROC_DEAD).
- `sched_loop` idle/shutdown — all flag reads are POLLS inside a 1ms-sleep
  loop, never parked waits, so staleness is bounded and no wake can be lost.
- Actor mailbox (`march_sched_send`/`recv`) — check-and-transition on both
  sides runs under `mbox_lock`; lock ordering substitutes for SC.
- `march_message.c` inbox — Treiber stack (single-location CAS push,
  exchange drain).
- RC ops — single-location `fetch_add/sub` with the documented returned-value
  ABA guard. HTTP date double-buffer — correct single-writer publish (reader
  keys on the flag stored LAST). FFI blocking-call `done` flag — same-location
  release/acquire message passing. Wake/park — the seq_cst + wake-permit
  protocol landed with the deadlock fix.

**FINDINGS — two, both filed, neither fixed here:**

- [ ] **HCR dispatch ring: `dlclose` runs BEFORE the `live=0` retire store**
  (`runtime/march_dispatch.c`, `march_dispatch_publish` reclaim path). The
  reader (`march_dispatch_enter`) does live-check -> pin (`refs` fetch_add) ->
  re-validate live, which is the right pattern — but the reclaimer's order is
  refs==0 check -> `slot_dlclose()` -> ... -> `live=0`. A reader that passed
  its live-check just before the refs check can pin and re-validate while
  dlclose is mid-flight; re-validation passes because `live=0` has not been
  stored yet, and the reader then calls a fn_ptr into an unloading `.so`.
  Plain TOCTOU (no weak memory needed). The in-code rationale — "acceptable
  under March's cooperative green-thread scheduler" — predates the
  multi-scheduler runtime and is stale. Cheap hardening: store `live=0`
  FIRST, then re-check `refs==0` (a pinned reader is now visible), and only
  then dlclose; full fix is the epoch/grace reclamation the comment already
  anticipates. Low urgency: HCR publish is an admin-path operation.
- [ ] **Signal.watch registration window can erase a delivery**
  (`runtime/march_runtime.c` watch/unwatch): registration stores the handler
  FIRST, then clears `pending`/`seen` — a signal arriving between the two has
  its `pending=1` wiped by the trailing clear, silently dropping that one
  delivery. Clear-then-install would close it. Adjacent to (but distinct
  from) the still-quarantined `signal_term_suppress` torn-output race.

Root causes (updated 2026-07-24):
- **the scheduler missed-wakeup deadlock** — FIXED (store-buffering memory-ordering
  bug + a residual wake-while-RUNNING drop). `task_burst_await` is back on `runtest`;
  the three node tests remain quarantined only on the shared-host port-collision
  verification blocker described in the table;
- **the `Signal.watch` dispatch race** (one test) — see its entry below. Note this one
  is NOT merely an ordering flake: the dominant failure shape is *torn* output, so an
  order-insensitive golden does not fix it.

`forge`'s is a separate, unrelated cause and is the only one of the five that is not a race.

**How you find out these are fixable:** `.github/workflows/nightly.yml`'s `quarantined`
job runs all six aliases every night, `continue-on-error`, purely as a signal — a green
run is the cue to un-quarantine (restore `(alias runtest)` in `test/dune`, or re-add
`test_build_check` to forge's `tests` stanza). **That lane reports; it does not fix, and
it does not gate anything.** Nothing else in CI runs these. Keep its alias list in sync
with the table above — before it existed, these tests ran in no workflow at all, which
makes a quarantine indistinguishable from a deletion.

## CI infra (2026-07-23)

- [ ] **The runtime-object cache served objects that predate a runtime source
  change — ROOT CAUSE NOT ESTABLISHED (2026-07-25).** After merging the
  `native_int_arr_map`/`native_float_arr_map` closure-ABI fix (which adds
  `native_float_arr_alloc_raw` to `runtime/march_runtime.c`),
  `bench/array_numeric.march` failed to LINK with
  `Undefined symbols: _native_float_arr_alloc_raw` — the symbol is present in
  the source and in the `_build` copy, so the *objects* being linked were
  stale. `MARCH_NO_RUNTIME_CACHE=1` links fine; `rm -rf
  ~/.march/cache/runtime-objs` fixes it permanently, and the gate has been
  green on the repopulated cache across repeated runs.

  **Why this should not have been possible, and therefore why it matters:**
  the key (`lib/cas/runtime_archive.ml`) already folds in
  `Cas.runtime_identity` (content digest of every `runtime/*.{c,h}`) AND
  `Cas.compiler_identity` (the march executable's bytes) — the latter
  precisely because `runtime_identity` resolves the runtime dir CWD-first and
  can digest the wrong tree. Both changed here, so the key should have
  changed and the entry should have missed. It did not. Until that is
  explained, the cache can silently serve objects that do not match the
  sources, and the failure mode is a link error at best and a wrong-behaviour
  binary at worst.

  Suspects worth checking first: the cache is GLOBAL and shared across every
  worktree, checkout and concurrent session (`~/.march/cache/runtime-objs`),
  so entries can be written by a process whose `runtime/` differs from the
  caller's; and a partially-populated key directory may be treated as a hit.
  Note the sibling open item below (key does not cover system-header
  CONTENTS) is a *different* gap in the same key.

  **Found by the bench gate**, within minutes of the runtime change landing —
  exactly the class of breakage that previously shipped unnoticed.

- [ ] **Runtime-object cache key doesn't cover system-header CONTENTS, only their `-I` paths and `-D` defines** (`lib/cas/runtime_archive.ml`, found in review 2026-07-24). `march_tls.c`/`march_compress.c`/`march_blake3.c` compile against openssl/zlib/zstd/brotli/blake3 headers discovered via `-I/opt/homebrew/opt/openssl@3/include` etc. A `brew upgrade openssl@3` (or a CI base-image libc bump that leaves clang's `--version` string unchanged) keeps the path, the define, `runtime_identity`, `compiler_identity` and `cc_identity` all identical, so the stale `.o` is served. Before the cache existed every build recompiled, so this self-healed silently. Real-world risk is moderate — OpenSSL 3.x and zlib are ABI-stable across minors, and a genuinely incompatible header change usually breaks the *link* loudly rather than miscompiling — but it is exactly the class of bug this project has been bitten by. Fix: fold `Digest.file` of the resolved `openssl/ssl.h`, `zlib.h`, `zstd.h`, `brotli/encode.h`, `blake3.h` into the key. Workaround today: `rm -rf ~/.march/cache/runtime-objs` after a system-library upgrade (or `MARCH_NO_RUNTIME_CACHE=1`).
- [ ] **`property-tests`' coverage guard validates against a hand-duplicated copy of the shard filters** (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`, found in review 2026-07-24). The guard's `COVERED` string is a second, manually-maintained copy of the three `matrix.include[].filter` values. It therefore catches a *newly added* property group (the failure mode it was written for) but NOT an *unwired* one: delete `algebraic oracle|` from the `rest` filter while leaving `COVERED` alone and the guard still passes, the `ran -eq 0` guard still passes (the shard runs its other groups), and 6 properties silently stop running. Fix: derive `COVERED` from the matrix itself, or have each shard emit the group names it actually matched and assert the union equals `test_properties.exe list`. (The analogous index-level gap in `property-oracle` IS now guarded — see the case-count tripwire in that job.)

- [ ] **QUARANTINED (2026-07-24): `test/native/signal_term_suppress` golden fails on a signal-dispatch race — NOT a simple ordering issue, sorting the golden does not fix it.** Reddened `test (ubuntu-24.04)` on 2026-07-24; the expected golden is `before term / survived term / term handler` (the handler is deferred to the scheduler drain after main's body). **Reproduced locally by looping the compiled binary hundreds to thousands of times: ~2-5% of runs fail**, and characterized the failure shape precisely (1000-run sample): only 9/1000 were a clean 3-line permutation (what a sorted diff, `node_discovery`'s approach, could tolerate) — the other 43/1000 were **TORN output**, e.g. `survived termterm handler` (the newline between the two lines is simply gone, not reordered elsewhere). A sorted-diff golden was considered and rejected: it would silently pass the torn-output majority right through as a "different sort order" mismatch (still failing, just for the wrong reason) and, worse, would validate the reordering minority as fine when this test's own doc comment asserts the order is supposed to be deterministic — sorting would mask a real ordering bug, not just tolerate benign nondeterminism. **Investigated the likely mechanism**: `march_println`/`march_print` (`runtime/march_runtime.c`) discard `writev`/`write`'s return value; `march_scheduler.c`'s own "Limitations / known EINTR exposure" comment documents that its SIGUSR1 preemption tick can EINTR a blocking syscall and that "on macOS SA_RESTART does not cover all syscalls" — a plausible short-write mechanism. **Implemented and tested a retry-on-short-write fix** (a `write`/`writev` retry loop in `march_print`/`march_println`) — it did NOT reduce the flake rate (28/500 with a guaranteed-fresh compile, `MARCH_NO_RUNTIME_CACHE=1` + caches cleared, so the runtime-object cache wasn't a confound). **Reverted that fix** (no measured benefit, and changing the hot-path print builtins without proof of effect isn't worth the risk) rather than ship unproven runtime code. Conclusion: the corruption happens **before** the write syscall — most likely the deferred `Signal.watch` dispatch (fired by whichever scheduler OS thread's per-iteration `march_signal_drain()` call happens to notice the pending flag first, not necessarily after the raising thread's own subsequent code has run) racing with the main green thread over something shared (allocator/GC), not a pure I/O interleaving issue. Same general bug *class* as the open scheduler-deadlock P0 below (heisenbug, not yet isolated to an exact missing sync edge) but a distinct symptom/repro — tracked separately here rather than folded in. **Quarantined** (`test/dune`, own alias `signal_term_suppress_quarantined`, matching the four scheduler-deadlock tests' pattern) rather than chased further, since isolating the actual race is a scheduler-level investigation out of scope for the CI-speedup work it surfaced during. Un-quarantine once the dispatch race is understood and fixed.

## `forge check`/`forge build` test suite unreliable in CI (2026-07-24)

- **OPEN — fourth, unrelated CI issue found while chasing the scheduler-deadlock/dune-daemon-wedge hangs above.** Even after quarantining all four scheduler-deadlock-prone tests, `build (ubuntu-24.04)`/`build (macos-15)` still failed: `forge/test/test_build_check.ml`'s "forge check"/"forge build" alcotest group (15 cases, shells out to a real `march check` subprocess via `forge/lib/cmd_build.ml`'s `check_all`) showed ~10 of 15 cases failing in CI on BOTH platforms — every case expecting success failed with the generic `Error "typecheck failed"`; every case expecting an `Error` result trivially "passed" since any failure satisfies it. That pattern (success-expecting cases uniformly fail, failure-expecting cases uniformly pass) is consistent with a blanket environmental failure of the underlying `march` invocation, not 10 independent bugs.
  - **NOT reliably reproduced locally.** A direct `dune build --root . @forge/test/runtest` run here shows only ONE real failure (case 8, see below) — 14/15 pass. A deliberately PATH-restricted local run (`env -i PATH=/usr/bin:/bin:/usr/sbin:/sbin`, confirmed via plain `/bin/sh -c 'which march'` that bare `march` is genuinely unresolvable) was hypothesized as the cause (`Toolchain.path_prefix ()` falls back to bare `"march"` — relying on ambient `PATH` — for an unpinned project, which every scaffolded test project here is; a fresh CI checkout never runs `dune install`, so `march` was assumed to never be on `PATH` there) but did NOT reproduce the cascade locally even under that restriction. The exact CI/local discrepancy is unexplained — worth investigating with the actual CI environment (or a container matching it) rather than guessing further locally.
  - **One of the 15 cases is a genuine, reproducible compiler bug, independent of the above:** "forge check 8" (`check: qualified-call cycle does not break bare ctor ordering`) fails to typecheck a `Core`/`Facade`/`Worker` 3-module qualified-call-cycle fixture with `I don't know a constructor called \`Bad\`` — `Core`'s own `type CoreError = Bad(String)` constructor isn't being found at all; the "did you mean" suggestion fuzzy-matches an unrelated stdlib type (`Bar` from `SeriesKind`) instead, confirming `Core.CoreError.Bad` truly isn't registered for this scenario rather than just misspelled. Plausibly a residual gap in the same-day module-resolution fix (commit `7849f0da`, "entry module can reference a same-name-prefixed dotted sibling") for 3-way qualified-call *cycles* specifically, distinct from the same-name-prefix sibling case that commit actually fixed.
  - **Mitigation:** quarantined the whole `test_build_check` binary off `runtest` (`forge/test/dune`, its own `build_check_quarantined` alias; compile-check untouched — the executable still builds, so a link/compile regression there still surfaces) rather than trying to reproduce+fix under time pressure. Costs coverage of 14 otherwise-passing forge tests until this is investigated. Runnable manually via `dune build @forge/test/build_check_quarantined`.

## P0 — Blocking / Active
- [ ] **Compiled `tcp_recv_http` panics "non-exhaustive pattern match" on a real read (2026-07-06)** — found while live-publishing `marathon` to production `forgepm.org` via the newly-wired `forge publish`. Minimal 15-line repro: `mod Main do needs IO.NetConnect fn main() do match tcp_connect(...) do Ok(fd) -> match tcp_send_all(fd, ...) do Ok(_) -> match tcp_recv_http(fd, 16777216) do ... end end end end` — compiled with `march --compile`, run against a real local HTTP server (not even TLS), panics inside the `tcp_recv_http(fd, ...)` call itself (never reaches the caller's own exhaustive `Err`/`Ok` match — confirmed via debug `println`s bisecting each step). Interpreted (`march file.march`, no `--compile`) does NOT panic on the identical program/server. `tcp_recv_http` is a native builtin (`march_tcp_recv_http`, declared `lib/tir/llvm_builtins.ml:430` — its `ret_ty`/`declare_sig` are already correctly `Result(String,String)`, unlike the `tls_write` bug below, so the cause is elsewhere in the compiled ADT-construction/match-compiler path). Blocks any compiled program from safely using plain (non-TLS) HTTP receive — does NOT block a real `https://` registry publish (that path uses `Tls.read`/`send_tls`, not `tcp_recv_http`/`send_plain`; confirmed fixed and working, see Done below). Root cause not yet found — needs a dedicated debugging session with `--emit-llvm` on the minimal repro.

## P1 — High Impact / Near-Term
- [ ] **Actor scheduler has ≥2 unbounded busy-spins that can peg a core at ~100% CPU forever under heavy host oversubscription — NOT live-confirmed, strong hypothesis only (investigated 2026-07-23).** Found while chasing why `test_erased_option_niche_fbip_no_underflow_compiled` (`test/test_codegen.ml:7668`, binary `erasedoptbin`) was observed hung for 3+ hours on a dev machine running several concurrent build/test sessions. `task_wait_done`'s in-scheduler branch (`runtime/march_runtime.c:1985`, what `task_await`/`task_await_unwrap` call) has **no timeout at all** — `while (task[4]==0) { march_sched_yield(); }` — unlike `Actor.call`'s own timed-wait path (`runtime/march_runtime.c:2340-2377`), which correctly bounds itself against `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`. `march_sched_wake`'s `PROC_PARKED` wait (`runtime/march_scheduler.c:1306`) is a second, tighter unbounded spin, justified in-comment as "O(1), extremely short" — true only if the target proc's OS thread is actually scheduled promptly, which isn't guaranteed under oversubscription (each compiled process spawns ~`nproc` scheduler OS threads via `MARCH_NUM_SCHEDULERS`; several concurrent march processes on one box easily oversubscribes real cores). Once either spin stalls, it adds CPU pressure that plausibly worsens the very starvation that caused it — a self-reinforcing pileup consistent with the multi-hour persistence and with a *different* test binary (`sanexit_bin`) independently observed stuck the same way. `march_sched_wait_idle` (`runtime/march_scheduler.c:1116-1148`) has the same shape but wasn't reached by this repro. **Not reproduced live**: ~160 attempts (200 sequential runs, then ~9 batches of 8 concurrent runs each with a 6s-grace lldb-attach watcher) all completed cleanly — this needs either a live capture or a careful structural fix mirroring `Actor.call`'s wall-clock-deadline pattern without changing `task_await`'s documented wait-forever semantics. Full writeup + reproduction recipe: memory `project_scheduler_unbounded_spin_hangs` (if accessible) or ask for the standalone fix-agent prompt drafted alongside this entry.

- [ ] **Revive the standalone C scheduler unit tests (`test/test_scheduler{,_mt}.c`).** They went DARK when the Signal.watch commit (`9c97391a`) added a `march_signal_drain()` call to `march_scheduler.c` — the standalone tests link ONLY `march_scheduler.c` (not the rest of the runtime that defines it), so they failed to LINK and `dune build` was silently red for weeks (unblocked 2026-07-18: a WEAK no-op `march_signal_drain` in `march_scheduler.c`, overridden by the runtime's strong def). Once they link, RUNNING them HANGS: `march_sched_run` now assumes the "main-as-green-thread" termination model (compiled `main` runs as a green thread whose return drives shutdown), but the tests spawn worker procs and call `march_sched_run()` from a plain C `main` with no main-green-thread, so `g_all_done` is never set and the loop idle-`nanosleep`s forever (confirmed via lldb: frame `march_sched_run` → `nanosleep`). Their RUN is disabled in `test/dune` (binaries still BUILD as a compile-check); the scheduler itself is covered by every compiled actor integration test. Revival = update the harness to the current termination contract (spawn work, then drive shutdown / mark the main proc done), then re-enable the `(alias runtest)` rules.

### Differential-oracle coverage follow-ups (Task 6 / Phase 3, 2026-07-04)
- [ ] **Widen the oracle opt-matrix with a `--no-opt` (TIR-optimizer-off) axis.** *(2026-07-15, open-items plan Phase 6.2 — PLUMBING LANDED, axis DISABLED: `oracle_check` gained a `?tir_opt` flag that adds `--no-opt`, but enabling the `--no-opt` config in `prop_oracle_opt_matrix` HANGS the property suite — a generated program compiled `--no-opt` runs under the scheduler, whose worker threads inherit `run_capture`'s pipe write end, so the read never returns even after the 10s run-timeout kills the direct child (the documented `march --compile | tail` pipe wedge). The config is left commented out; re-enabling it requires hardening `run_capture` to use a file redirect instead of pipe capture. Note: `test/test_properties.ml` is NOT run by `scripts/run-tests.sh`.)* Task 6's `prop_oracle_opt_matrix` (`test/test_properties.ml`) diffs the interpreter against the compiled binary at `--opt 0` and `--opt 2` — but `--opt N` is the *clang* codegen level (`opt_level`, `bin/main.ml`), NOT the March/TIR optimization passes. The TIR-level optimizer-miscompile family (the sort_by/cprop class the plan cites) is gated by the SEPARATE `--no-opt` flag (`opt_enabled`, which toggles `Fusion.run`/`Opt.run` etc.). That family is already caught *as a flat mismatch* by the existing default-O2-vs-interpreter comparison (the default has TIR passes on), so this is coverage-broadening, not a gap: adding a third matrix configuration that compiles with `--no-opt` and diffs TIR-off-vs-interpreter (and, for attribution, TIR-off-vs-TIR-on) would (a) exercise the less-tested base-codegen path and (b) let a TIR-optimizer miscompile be *attributed* (agrees at `--no-opt`, diverges with passes on) rather than only flagged. Deferred from Task 6 to bound wall-clock and redden-risk (the `--no-opt` path is less exercised; a base-codegen divergence there would need filing + carving before it can land green). When added, reuse the existing `oracle_check ~opt` plumbing — extend it to also thread `--no-opt`, or add a parallel `?tir_opt` flag.

See the corpus-scope comment at the top of `test/test_oracle.ml` for the full rationale. (Moved here from P0 — this is test-infra coverage-broadening, not a blocking compiler bug.) **PARTIALLY DONE (2026-07-15):** part (a) landed — a curated `test_native_allowlist` of 15 deterministic, self-contained `test/native/` programs (closures/niches/tuple+newtype layout/let-shadowing/default-args/qualified+nested-module calls) is now swept in the native lane, each verified MATCH interp==compiled (77 MATCH total, up from 62). ## P2 — Important / Near-Term
### Compiler: Capabilities/effects (found during Core March widening slice 5)
- [ ] **F1 — body-scanned IO caps are WARNING-only, not error; "absence of `needs` = machine-verified purity" holds only when authors thread `Cap(X)` through signatures.** Found and pinned by `specs/lang/core-march-types.md` §2.8.6 (capabilities widening, Task 2, 2026-07-07). Check 1b (`typecheck.ml:5588-5605`, `Err.warning_with_fix` at `:5596`) — a function BODY calling a `builtin_cap_table` builtin (`file_read`, `tls_connect`, `vault_set`, …) without a covering `needs` — is WARNING-only, not an error: `--check` exits 0. Contrast Check 1 (signature `Cap(X)`), Check 4 (transitive `use`), and Check 5 (extern `Cap(X)`), which are all true ERRORs, exit 1. So a module that calls IO builtins directly in a function body, never threading `Cap(X)` through any signature, is *warned*, not *rejected* — the guarantee "no `needs` declared ⇒ the function is pure" only holds at the ERROR-level signature/transitive/extern-cap surface, not at the body-call surface. `specs/lang/capabilities.md` overclaims this: `:69` ("The compiler enforces this transitively... the build fails"), and the prose framing around `:142`/`:312` ("There are no false positives"; "the type checker already enforces `needs` as an error") describes the body-call case as effectively error-level when it is warning-level in the actual implementation (the example text in those sections does correctly say "Warning"/"hint", but the surrounding claims overstate the guarantee). Verified live, both sides reproduced side by side: a `Cap(IO.FileRead)` signature with no `needs` rejects (`` `Cap(IO.FileRead)` used in module `Reader` but `IO.FileRead` is not declared in `needs`. ``, exit 1); a body call to `file_read` with no `needs` only emits a HINT + WARNING (`` function body calls a builtin that requires `Cap(IO.FileRead)` but `Reader` does not declare `needs IO.FileRead`. ``, exit 0). This is a design/enforcement gap, not a mechanical bug — Checks 1/4/5 themselves are sound and precise over the `builtin_cap_table` domain. Filed here (not fixed — docs-only widening slice); reconciling `capabilities.md`'s wording against this reality is deferred to Task 7 of the widening plan. See `specs/lang/core-march-types.md` §2.8.6 for the full three-tier table and both live transcripts.
### Compiler: let? / Result-propagation (found during Core March widening slice 8, 2026-07-10)
- [ ] **The tutorial's dedicated "`let?` cannot have a type annotation" parser error production was never implemented — `let? x : T = e` is rejected by the GENERIC missing-`=` recovery instead.** `let-propagation.md` §5.2 shows a planned `LET; QUESTION; simple_pattern; COLON; error` production emitting "`let?` bindings cannot have a type annotation — the type is inferred from the Result:". That production is absent from `parser.mly` (only `LET QUESTION simple_pattern EQUALS expr` and `LET QUESTION simple_pattern error` exist). So `let? x : Int = e` hits the generic `error` recovery and reports `` I was expecting `=` in the let? binding here: `` — correct REJECTION, less-specific MESSAGE. Live-verified during slice 8; reject witness `types/reject/t70_letq_type_annotation` pins the actual (generic) text. Fix (optional, cosmetic): add the dedicated COLON error production for a clearer diagnostic. Tutorial reconciled (§2.10.2 + let-propagation.md implementation note both record the deviation). NOT a soundness issue.

### Compiler docs: Perceus RC + Refinement types (Core March widening slices 11 + 12, 2026-07-11)
- [ ] **Perceus/RC discipline: FBIP/reuse and atomic RC mode-selection deliberately excluded from §4.16, pending more groundwork.** `core-march.md` §4.16 states only the RC-applicability divergence (`needs_rc`/`borrow_eligible`) and the dual-position dup/drop invariant (B1) as operational rules, both already backed by a committed TIR snapshot AND now a golden. Excluded on purpose: (1) FBIP/reuse (`lib/tir/perceus_fbip.ml`) needs a "reuse preserves semantics" theorem to state as a real premise/conclusion rule — today's arity-compatibility check proves size-compatibility, not full semantic equivalence, so writing a rule would overclaim; (2) atomic RC mode-selection (`specs/atomic-rc-design.md`) is an undesigned DRAFT — no `rc_mode` field, no `lib/tir/rc_mode.ml`, no `_local`/atomic runtime variant-selection exists in the compiler today (only the always-on `march_incrc_local`/`march_decrc_local` fast path), so there is nothing implemented to document as a rule. Both are natural next widening slices once the underlying compiler work lands — not gaps in THIS slice's honesty, just explicitly out of scope.

### Compiler: Refinement types honest-limitation note (Core March widening slice 12, 2026-07-11)
- [ ] **Not a bug, filed for visibility: some higher-order shapes bypass refinement checking, pinned by a passing accept witness rather than tutorial prose.** (**Narrowed 2026-07-25** — the blanket "direct-calls-only" framing below is no longer accurate. Two higher-order shapes ARE now checked: a call through a *refined* callback type (`f : ({Int | _ >= 0}) -> Int`) and a call through a local alias (`let g = take_n  g(-3)`). The `t77` witness stays valid because it uses an *unrefined* callback type `f : Int -> Int` with an unrefined `x`, so nothing at the call site states the requirement — catching that needs either contravariant subtyping at the pass site or inferring `apply`'s own requirement from its body, both still open. Confirmed still exit 0 after the higher-order work landed.) `accept/t77_refine_hof_bypass_limitation` proves `apply(take_n, -3)` — calling a `{Int | _ >= 0}`-refined function INDIRECTLY through a higher-order parameter — passes `--check` even though the identical literal at a direct call site (`reject/t71`) is rejected. This was already documented in `specs/lang/refinement-types.md`'s "Limitations" section before this slice; the slice's contribution is making it a mechanically-checked corpus fact (so a future change that silently narrows or widens this boundary shows up as a corpus regression) rather than only a prose claim. No runtime check is promised for ANY refinement (core-march-types.md §2.14's erasure rule), so an unverified indirect call is merely *unverified*, never *unsafe* — this is a completeness gap in the static analysis, not a soundness hole.

### Compiler: Parallelism + Clustering (found during Core March widening slices 9 + 10, 2026-07-10)
- [ ] **P1 — `Parallel.psum_float` is not backend-portable and must not be golden-compared.** IEEE-754 `+.` is not associative, so `preduce`'s differing chunk counts (the interpreter's worker count comes from `Domain.recommended_domain_count`, the compiled runtime's from the physical CPU count, and both differ across machines) can reorder the additions and change the last bit. The same caveat applies to any `preduce` with a non-associative `merge`. `psum_float` has no doc comment flagging this; the existing `test_rrb_vec.march` unit test happens to use binary-exact inputs (`[1.0,2.5,3.5]==7.0`) so it passes, but that is luck, not a guarantee. Deliberately EXCLUDED from golden `g43` (documented in `core-march.md` §4.14 + the golden's header). Fix options (optional): add a doc-comment warning on `psum_float`, or pin it to binary-exact float inputs. NOT a soundness bug — a documented non-portability. See §4.14.

### Compiler: Linearity (found during Core March widening slice 7, 2026-07-10)
- [ ] **ARCHITECTURE — Fully-qualified type & constructor identity (flat-namespace overhaul).** **SPEC'D (2026-07-17): `specs/plans/2026-07-17-fqn-type-ctor-identity.md`.** Types/ctors resolve by BARE name today (`surface_ty`'s `canon_name` collapses qualified→bare, `typecheck.ml:2672`; `TCon` carries a bare name; `env.types`/`env.ctors` are flat maps), so two modules' same-named types are literally one nominal type — while VALUES already resolve toward qualified identity + visibility (`qualify_module_refs`, `pub_set`). The spec finishes that inconsistency: FQN canonical identity for types/ctors, bare/aliased refs resolved to it, with a local-shadows-import + ambiguous-bare-is-an-error rule under `MARCH_LIB_PATH` auto-discovery. Staged (spike → carry-identity → resolution-rules → flip-consumers), with hot-path (unification) + mangling/CAS + per-file-typecheck risks called out. **This is the shared upstream blocker for L4, the impl-coherence orphan rule, the ctor cross-talk, and the opaque-type asymmetry — fixing it once unblocks all four.** Four open sign-off questions (behavior-rule scope, mangling re-baseline, explicit-imports, sequencing-vs-stopgap).

- **Cross-ref:** session-channel linearity's own gaps (parameter-endpoint reuse; unclosed-`SEnd` drop) are the pre-existing **F7**, filed under "Compiler: Session types" below — the generic tracker documented in §2.9 is the ONLY session-linearity enforcement, so F7's shapes are exactly the shapes this tracker cannot see.

### Compiler: Session types (protocols/channels)
- [ ] **F6 — SEMANTICS (scope boundary, not a bug to fix): the channel runtime has no scheduler, so a `recv` called before its matching `send` in program order typechecks cleanly but deadlocks/aborts at runtime on BOTH backends.** Found and pinned by `specs/lang/core-march.md` §4.11 and §4.1 finding 23 (session-types widening, Task 4, 2026-07-06). The channel runtime is synchronous, single-threaded, non-blocking: `chan_recv` (`eval.ml:2655`) does not suspend when its `ce_in_q` queue is empty — it raises a hard error immediately (`eval.ml:2660`, `"Chan.recv: channel %s#%d has no pending value — did you run the sender first?"`); there is no coroutine/actor-mailbox integration for channels, so both endpoints of a session must run in the same straight-line thread with every `send` textually ordered before its matching `recv`. Verified live (this task): a program calling a `server_side` function (whose FIRST op is `Chan.recv`) before a `client_side` function (whose `Chan.send` would have satisfied it) — a protocol that typechecks (`--check` exit 0, session-state advancement is purely a static per-op check, oblivious to call order) — dies at runtime on both backends: interpreted, `Chan.recv: channel Echo#0 has no pending value — did you run the sender first?` (exit 1); compiled, `march: Chan.recv on empty channel queue (role 1)` then abort (exit 134). Neither the type system nor any runtime scheduler prevents the ordering mistake; both backends simply agree it is a fatal error once it happens. **This is a documented scope boundary, not a defect to fix:** session types here are a linear protocol-*conformance* checker layered over a same-thread mailbox, not a concurrent-session / coroutine system — the type system guarantees "the protocol steps you take advance a valid session state," not "your calls into that protocol are scheduled in the order the protocol requires." Filed here for reference-completeness (no fix proposed; a scheduler/coroutine integration would be a substantial feature, out of scope for a docs-widening task). See `specs/lang/core-march.md` §4.11 for the full writeup and both live-verified backend error messages.
- [ ] **Session types: make "awaiting `offer` refinement" a session STATE rather than a side table (durable form of the Finding 1 fix above, OPEN).** Today `env.offer_unrefined` is a physical-identity list guarded at two chokepoints. The structural fix is a new `session_ty` constructor — `SOfferPending of (string * session_ty) list` — that `Chan.offer` puts the returned ref at when the branches diverge, and that only `with_offer_refinement` strips. It would then survive unification, annotations, and function boundaries BY CONSTRUCTION rather than by a checked invariant, and would need no `unify` special case at all. Deferred because it touches `pp_session_ty`, `session_ty_equal`, `session_ty_exact_equal`, `dual_session_ty`, `unfold_srec`, `subst_svar`, and every `match` over `session_ty` — many of which have `_` fallbacks, so OCaml's exhaustiveness warnings would NOT enumerate the sites for you. Do it with a survey of every `session_ty` match first, not by chasing compiler errors.

### Runtime: Hot Code Reloading
- [ ] **Phase 0 — Spike (MANDATORY before any Model B work)** — Throwaway prototype proving native code can be swapped across a boundary without memory corruption: two app modules with a `Conn`-style borrowed-record boundary call, frozen RC/borrow ABI, hot-swap callee, run under ASan → zero double-frees/leaks; introduce a new closure shape flowing through the boundary → prove a no-defun-at-boundary scheme works (no defun fault) or conclude Model B can't defunctionalize the boundary; measure the boundary deoptimization on `bench/list_ops.march` + a Bastion request bench. Deliverable: go/no-go on Model B with real numbers. (Model A needs no spike.)
- [ ] **Phase 6 — Model B: native JIT upgrade (gated on Phase 0 go)** — ORC JIT both platforms (pinned thread; macOS `MAP_JIT` + `pthread_jit_write_protect_np` + `allow-jit` entitlement note). **ORC end-to-end, NO `dlopen`** (ORC produces in-memory symbols, not an ELF `.so` — conflated in the first draft). Background upgrade of trampolined SCCs to native O2 (keyed by the same `impl_hash`); no-defun-at-boundary scheme per the spike; measured perf.
- [ ] **Audit log doesn't record capability data (found reviewing `docs/hot-code-reload.md` for accuracy, 2026-07-24)** — `write_audit_log` (`runtime/march_reload.c`) writes one JSON line per `ACTIVATE` with `ts/type/fn/impl_hash/signer/cas_hash/result` (per Phase 7 above) — no capability field at all. So today the audit log answers "who deployed this function, when, and did it succeed," but not "what capabilities did this deploy touch" — a `--grant-cap`-authorized widening isn't distinguishable, after the fact, from an ordinary same-authority redeploy, and there's no way to reconstruct a capability-history timeline for an actor/function from the log alone (the granted/declared caps live only in the signed `.hcr_manifest`/wire payload at deploy time, not in any durable per-activation record). Add a `caps`/`cap_root` field (and, for the client-side gate, which `--grant-cap` flags were used) to the audit line so an operator can answer "when did this system last gain capability X" from the audit log directly instead of needing the manifest from that specific deploy.
## P3 — Medium-Term
### Language Features
- [ ] **External function interface (C-first ABI) — remaining gaps.** The 9-phase C-first FFI (ABI core, string/Option/Result marshalling, `resource`/`consume`, interpreter dlopen path, forge `[ffi]` build integration, `blocking fn`, `gen-c` codegen, record/variant/Option/Result/List codecs, native→March upcalls, the Rust `march` crate layer) is DONE — see `specs/2026-06-19-c-ffi-abi-design.md`, `specs/c-ffi-rust-layer.md`, and `specs/c-ffi-gaps.md` for the full history. Still open: `forge ffi import` (C header → extern block, needs libclang), recursive/generic types in generated codecs (currently passthrough), multi-lib `[[ffi]]` (array-of-tables, blocked on a TOML parser limitation — also blocks `[[ffi.rust]]` and Rust crate publishing beyond path-only), and an OCaml FFI layer analogous to the Rust one (designed-for but not built).
- [ ] **MCP server for March** — An MCP (Model Context Protocol) server that exposes March's type checker and compiler as tools for LLM agents. Post-LSP, pre-1.0. Would expose: typecheck a snippet, get type at position, search by type signature, expand typed holes.
## P4 — Long-Term / Research
### Metatheory
- [ ] **Lean 4 mechanized metatheory** — Proof of type safety (progress + preservation) for the core March type system. Plan documented in `specs/plans/lean4-metatheory-plan.md`. Requires: formalized grammar, operational semantics, type rules, mechanized proofs of canonical forms lemma, substitution lemma, and soundness theorem.

### Compiler Backend
- [ ] **Query-based/demand-driven compiler architecture** — Current pipeline is linear (parse → desugar → typecheck → eval/TIR). Design goal is a query-based architecture (like `rustc`'s `salsa`) for fine-grained incremental recompilation. Deferred post-v1.
### Compiler Optimizations (Planned)
- [ ] **P1 Layer 2 — non-leading / interleaved common lets** — Today only the *first* let of each arm is peeled. Generalize to a common pure binding anywhere in an arm's leading let-chain whose RHS does not depend on arm-specific earlier bindings, with reordering. Medium. See `specs/optimizations.md` §P1.
- [ ] **P1 Layer 3 — general non-let-bound cross-arm CSE** — Hoist shared sub-expressions ANF did not already lift to a head let; gate on `Purity.is_pure`. Mostly subsumed by ANF, low marginal value. See `specs/optimizations.md` §P1.
- [ ] **P8 Layer 2 — delayed/non-immediate FBIP reuse** — Current cross-tag reuse only fires when destruction and reconstruction are adjacent; extend to reuse across intervening code. Medium-High. See `specs/optimizations.md` §P8.

## Done (recently completed)

- ✅ **The compiled HTTP server is covered end-to-end by the automated suite (2026-07-31, new `test/test_http_native.ml`, wired via `test/dune` + `test/run_stdlib.ml`).** An outage of the compiled `HttpServer` (panic on request 1, segfault on request 2) went unnoticed because `test/test_http_native.sh`, the only e2e test of it, was referenced by no dune rule and no CI workflow — and compiled without `--opt`, made one request per server, and asserted only on status codes, so it would have passed against both a silently-skipped pipeline and the request-2 crash. The replacement rides `run_stdlib.exe`, so `dune runtest` and CI's `test` job run it on macOS and Linux; alone: `./_build/default/test/run_stdlib.exe test 'http server' -e`. Compiled at `--opt 2`, ~65 requests per process across fresh connections + keep-alive + a pipelined burst, body assertions with two routes echoing request-derived data, an explicit liveness assertion decoding `128 + signal`, and both server implementations as peers. Ephemeral ports, bounded waits, and the only skip is clang genuinely absent. **Verified non-vacuous:** with the `march_incrc_local(pipeline)` fix removed and the runtime honestly restaged, both cases FAIL with `exited with status 139 (= 128 + signal 11)`. `run_stdlib` 826 → 828.

- ✅ **The thread-pool HTTP server starved connections past `pool_size` — fixed (2026-07-31, `runtime/march_http.c` `pool_grow_if_starved`).** A worker owns a connection for its entire keep-alive lifetime, so a fixed pool of N workers served at most N concurrent connections and the rest were accepted by the kernel and never read — at 256 offered connections with the default `ncpus*2` = 28 workers, 228 sat with unread bytes in `Recv-Q` indefinitely. The pool is now elastic: `size` is a floor, and a worker about to park starts one more if it was the last idle one, up to `max_size` (from `HttpServer.max_connections`, which until now was accepted and discarded). `threads` is allocated at `max_size` upfront so growth never reallocates a live array, and `pool_stop` joins a `size` snapshot taken under the queue lock that `pool_grow_if_starved` also holds. **Measured at c=256, order-swapped and repeated: 228 unread → 0, in-flight 27.7 → 253.7.** Reported latency rises 0.90 → 8.67 ms, which is the honest number: the old average covered only the 28 connections being served. Throughput −5% (30,774 → 29,261) because the box's ~30k rps ceiling is client/kernel-side, so 9× the connections buys no throughput and costs scheduler overhead. Above a few hundred concurrent connections the event loop (`MARCH_HTTP_EVLOOP=1`) remains the better answer at 15–17% less CPU per request.

- ✅ **`march_response_send_plaintext` deleted (2026-07-31, `runtime/march_http_response.{c,h}`).** A TechEmpower `/plaintext` fast path hardcoding `Content-Length: 13` and the literal body `"Hello, World!"`; reaching it required bypassing the user's March router, so the March program nominally under benchmark would never execute. Zero callers, ever. The header now records why a general small-fixed-response path is also not worth adding — the normal builder is already zero-copy.

- ✅ **`specs/features/http-and-networking.md` no longer calls the event loop the default (2026-07-31).** It has been opt-in behind `MARCH_HTTP_EVLOOP=1` since it was made so; the doc now describes the thread pool as the default with its blocking-handler rationale, the event loop as opt-in with its no-blocking-I/O constraint and 15–17% CPU advantage, and carries a note that the previous text was wrong.


- ✅ **A `(`-led statement was glued onto the previous line as a call — a compiled program crashed with `EXC_BAD_ACCESS`/exit 138 before running any user code (2026-07-30, `lib/parser/token_filter.ml` `emit`, `lib/parser/parser.mly` new `LPAREN_STMT` token).** Reported as native-codegen mis-lowering: a function that both discarded a parameter (`let _ = a`) and returned a literal `()` was never emitted at all, and its call site had become a closure-style indirect call *through its own argument* — `getelementptr i8, ptr %sl, i64 16` (the closure ABI's `fn_ptr` slot) followed by `call ptr (ptr) %fv(%sl)`, with `%sl` a `String`, landing the program counter on `0x1`. Codegen was faithful: `--dump-tir` at the `tir-lower` stage already showed the callee's body as `a()`. Inside a block the token filter swallows newlines, so `let _ = a` followed by a line holding only `()` reached menhir as `a` `(` `)` adjacent and took the call rule — the *parameter was being invoked*. The interpreter confirmed it independently (passing `fn -> IO.puts("CALLED")` printed `CALLED`; passing a String raised "applied non-function value"), which is what collapsed a two-fault codegen theory into a one-line parse fact. The reported trigger matrix follows directly: the discard is what leaves a value-ending token at the end of one line and the literal `()` is what puts a `(` at the start of the next, so removing either fixes it, a `None`/`0`/`IO.puts(…)` tail has no leading `(`, and a zero-arg callee has no discard. **Not** cross-module (a same-module callee reproduces identically) and **not** an RC bug. **Fix:** the newline-separates-statements rule was already specified for `f(1)`⏎`(g(2))` (grammar §7.3, witness `parse/p24`) but was enforced only for the curried-call `)`⏎`(` shape — the *classification* of the paren ignored the newline. A `(` following a value-ending token across a newline is now retagged `LPAREN_STMT`, accepted only by `expr_atom`'s group/tuple/unit rules and `simple_pattern`'s parenthesised/tuple rules (a match arm may open a fresh line with a tuple pattern — the `Deque.pop_front` codegen test caught that mid-fix), never by the call rule. menhir's shift/reduce count is unchanged at 9. **Pinned by** four `run_compiler` parse tests including two negative controls (a same-line call and a multi-line argument list must both still parse as calls), two `run_codegen` tests (no `call ptr (ptr)` emitted for the program; the binary compiles, prints `ok`, exits 0), and grammar witness `parse/p33_paren_stmt_after_bare_ident.march` — all confirmed RED against a `git show`-sourced pre-fix parser and GREEN after. Suites: `run_compiler` 619, `run_codegen` 520, `run_eval` 256, `run_snapshots` 33 unchanged, `run_stdlib` 826 with only the pre-existing environmental `MARCH_SANITIZE` failure, grammar 45/45.

- ✅ **Static capture-free closures — one immortal global per top-level function value instead of a per-materialization heap allocation (2026-07-28, `lib/tir/llvm_ctx.ml` `intern_static_closure` + `static_clos` memo, both closure-materialization sites in `lib/tir/llvm_emit.ml`).** A top-level function materialized as a first-class value (`let f = some_fn`, passed as a callback, stored in a list/tuple, …) whose closure captures nothing was allocated fresh via `march_alloc(i64 24)` at *every* materialization, every time it executed, and never freed via normal RC — an unbounded per-materialization leak in any loop that repeatedly took a function value. Fixed by emitting one `internal global` per top-level function used as a value (`@<fn>$static_clo`, LLVM type `{i64,i32,i32,ptr}` = refcount, tag, `march_hdr.pad`, code pointer, refcount pre-set to `MARCH_RC_IMMORTAL` = 1099511627776), memoized by function name so repeated materializations resolve to the same global and distinct functions get distinct globals. Ordinary RC inc/dec code at call sites still executes against the global (e.g. `march_incrc_local` directly against `@<fn>$static_clo`) — those are atomic read-modify-writes on a now module-wide-shared cache line that never drive the refcount to zero, i.e. **never-freeing, not no-ops** (no evidence of contention: an 8-task `Task.async` workload over a shared closure showed correct results, 0.33s, 52 allocs, no RC underflow, but the multi-thread-contention question is not foreclosed by this fix). Declared mutable (`internal global`, not `constant`) because those routine RC stores must remain valid; a `constant` global would make them undefined behavior. **Measured** (4,000,000 materializations of a named function value, compiled `--opt 2`): `obj_allocs` 4,000,000 → 0, peak RSS 125.4 MB → 2.9 MB, wall 0.09s → 0.01s — matching a control that calls the function directly with no value materialization at all. **Gated OFF** (via a shared `static_closure_ok` predicate) for the REPL/JIT (`ctx.repl` — each REPL eval compiles/links a fresh module, so a baked-in global can't be safely shared or discarded across the incremental-compilation lifecycle) and hot-reload boundary functions (`ctx.hr_config` + `Hot_reload.is_reloadable` — defensive, not a fix for a demonstrated staleness bug: the fresh-alloc fallback bakes in the identical `@<fn>$clo_wrap` pointer, and `clo_wrap_define` already emits a hardcoded direct `call @<fn>` that bypasses the versioned HCR dispatch table regardless of path — a pre-existing, already-documented HCR gap this change doesn't touch; the exclusion keeps this change strictly non-regressive for HCR rather than claiming to fix that gap). **Does NOT cover capturing closures** — see the new open item below, found during this same work and deliberately left unfixed here since it needs a genuine `perceus.ml`/`borrow.ml` ownership fix, not a codegen change. **At the time of this entry, also did not cover capture-free LAMBDA values** (as opposed to top-level named functions) — since fixed, see the "Static capture-free LAMBDA values" Done entry above. Verified via `llvm_builtins_preamble_golden` (static-global-replaces-alloc, REPL-mode exclusion via `emit_repl_expr`, and REPL-mode exclusion via `emit_fns_fragment ~repl:true`), a compiled/interpreted parity fixture proving function-value semantics are unchanged (`test/native/static_closure_semantics.march`) plus the runtime-live-allocs growth assertion in `test/test_codegen.ml` (`test_static_closure_materialization_no_leak_compiled`), and a compiled/interpreted parity fixture proving no cross-function global collision (`test/native/static_closure_semantics.march`, 3 distinct `$static_clo` globals for 3 distinct functions). Benchmark controls (`bench/list_ops.march`, `bench/binary_trees.march`, neither of which materializes a named top-level function as a value) ran clean, no measurable before/after difference — see `specs/progress.md` for the numbers.

- ✅ **JIT REPL-mode gate for static closures was incomplete — `precompile_stdlib`'s `emit_fns_fragment` call omitted `~repl:true` (2026-07-28, `lib/jit/repl_jit.ml:823`, found in final whole-branch review of the static-closures work above).** `static_closure_ok`'s REPL/JIT exclusion depends on `ctx.repl`, which every `emit_fns_fragment`/`emit_repl_*` call site must opt into by passing `~repl:true`. Two of the three `emit_fns_fragment` call sites in `repl_jit.ml` did; `precompile_stdlib` (the JIT's stdlib-prelude fragment, warmed once per content-hash and cached to `~/.cache/march/stdlib_prelude_O1_*.so`) did not, silently falling back to `Llvm_repl.emit_fns_fragment`'s `?(repl : bool = false)` default. Proven with a cold cache (`HOME` override): the resulting `.so` contained 13 `$static_clo` symbols (e.g. `_Cluster.parse_addr$static_clo`), contradicting `specs/optimizations.md`/`specs/progress.md`/this file's claim that the optimization is gated off for the REPL/JIT. The observed behavior was benign in practice (the globals are `internal`/non-external so JITDylib-collision can't fire, they live in writable `__DATA`, and the prelude `.so` is never unloaded mid-session) — but the invariant was enforced by linkage luck, not by code. **Fixed** by passing `~repl:true` at the call site, and by removing the `?(repl = false)` default entirely (`lib/tir/llvm_repl.ml`'s `emit_fns_fragment` now takes `~repl:bool` unconditionally, `lib/tir/llvm_emit.ml`'s wrapper likewise) so every future call site must state its REPL-mode explicitly — this turns the same bug class into a compile error instead of a silent default. **Verified**: rebuilt the JIT stdlib prelude cold (fresh `HOME`) before and after — `nm -m <so> | grep -c static_clo` went from 13 to 0. **Regression added**: `test_static_closure_not_emitted_in_fns_fragment_repl_mode` (`test/test_codegen.ml`) drives `Llvm_emit.emit_fns_fragment ~repl:true` directly — the exact function `precompile_stdlib` calls — with a fn materialized as a value inside the fragment, and asserts no `$static_clo` global appears (previously only `emit_repl_expr` had REPL-mode coverage).

- ✅ **Static capture-free LAMBDA values — extended the static-closure mechanism above to anonymous lambdas, not just top-level named functions (2026-07-28, `lib/tir/llvm_emit.ml`'s `EAlloc` arm).** Previously `static_closure_ok`/`intern_static_closure` only fired for a top-level `fn`-declared function materialized as a value; an anonymous lambda expression that captures nothing, passed as a value at its own creation site (e.g. `apply_it(fn x -> x * 2, i)`), was neither a top-level named function nor a capturing closure — it fell between the two and kept allocating fresh every materialization, never freed. Minimal repro, compiled `--opt 2`:
  ```march
  mod Main do
    fn apply_it(f : Int -> Int, n : Int) : Int do f(n) end

    fn go(i : Int, acc : Int) : Int do
      if i <= 0 do
        acc
      else
        go(i - 1, acc + apply_it(fn x -> x * 2, i))
      end
    end

    fn main() : () do
      println(go(4000000, 0))
    end
  end
  ```
  **Fixed** by recognizing this shape at LLVM emission with no `defun.ml` change needed — the discriminator is derivable purely at the emission site: `defun.ml` lifts every lambda to `EAlloc (TCon (clo_name, []), fn_ptr_atom :: fv_atoms)`, so a lambda that captures nothing leaves `fv_atoms = []`, i.e. the `EAlloc` carries **exactly one** argument (the lifted apply function's address, `Tir_names.is_clo_struct tcon_name` confirms it's a closure struct). That shape routes to the same `intern_static_closure` interning path a top-level function value uses. No `$clo_wrap` trampoline is generated for the lambda case (unlike the earlier bug where `clo_wrap_define` mattered) — the lifted apply function defunctionalization produces already has the `(clo_ptr, params…)` closure-call ABI, so field 0 can point straight at it. **Gated** the same way as the named-function case for REPL/JIT (`not ctx.repl`), but *more conservatively* for hot-reload: rather than resolving the defunctionalized closure's synthetic type name (`"$Clo_" ^ fn_name ^ "$" ^ uid`, not always unambiguously demangled — it may itself embed `$`) back to an owning module via `Hot_reload.is_reloadable`, static lambdas are disabled outright whenever `ctx.hr_config` is set at all. **Measured** (`MARCH_STRING_STATS=1`, `--opt 2`, 4,000,000 iterations of `apply_it(fn x -> x * 2, i)`): `obj_allocs` 4,000,000 → 0, peak RSS ~131.5 MB → ~2.99 MB. Compiled and interpreted output byte-identical before and after (no observable-behavior change, only the allocation strategy). No wall-clock speedup is claimed beyond the allocation/RSS elimination — this was not benchmarked for wall-clock. **Still does NOT cover capturing closures** — see the next open item; a capturing lambda's `EAlloc` always carries 2+ arguments (code pointer plus at least one captured free variable) and so never matches this single-argument shape, by design, since its contents differ per instance and cannot be a module-lifetime static object. Verified non-vacuous (disabling the new arm reproduces `LEAKED 20000`-shaped unbounded growth; restoring it gives bounded growth) via `test_lambda_static_closure_materialization_no_leak_compiled` (`test/test_codegen.ml`, "compiled capture-free lambda materialization does not leak per use") plus a native golden `test/native/static_lambda_no_leak.march` covering two distinct capture-free lambdas (4 distinct `$static_clo` globals confirmed, none shared) and a capture-free lambda alongside a capturing one in the same function, confirming the discriminator separates the two cases correctly at runtime.

- ✅ **Capturing closures leak one allocation per materialization (compiled) — FIXED 2026-07-29** (`lib/tir/borrow.ml` `infer_module`'s `init` pins apply-fn param 0 to owned; `lib/tir/perceus.ml` `insert_apply_fn_clo_drop` emits the matching callee-side release). Root cause: TWO independent notions of "$clo is borrowed" — the borrow map (what callers consult in Perceus's `EApp` case) and the per-function `borrowed` set (what suppresses the callee's drop) — disagreed, so the caller deferred to the callee and the callee deferred to the borrow map. A prior callee-only attempt produced 3 double-frees plus 8 stdlib crashes precisely because it flipped only the second. Measured: 4M-iteration loop 4,000,000 allocations / ~125 MB peak RSS → ~2.9 MB floor; `live_allocs` growth over 20,000 materializations 20,000 → bounded (`test_capturing_closure_materialization_no_leak_compiled`, verified non-vacuous against origin/main which reports `LEAKED 20000`). The drop is deliberately NOT emitted for capture-free apply fns: natively their closure is the immortal static global where a decrement is a no-op, but under the REPL/JIT (`static_closure_ok` is `not ctx.repl && ..`) it is a real allocation, and dropping it made `run_codegen`'s "stdlib List.length via precompile" jump through a zeroed apply-fn slot (EXC_BAD_ACCESS at 0x0, frame #0 = 0x0) while every native program stayed green. **Residual — FIXED 2026-07-29 (same-day follow-up).** A SELF-RECURSIVE capturing
closure leaked one allocation per top-level MATERIALIZATION (not per recursion
level — the whole recursion reuses one heap object via the self-binding alias).
`insert_apply_fn_clo_drop`'s single unconditional early drop cancels the
self-binding's protective `inc_rc $clo` (net zero) but never releases the
actual transferred reference, which flows unchanged through the recursion and
is abandoned at any base-case branch that never uses the self-binding again.
Fixed with a SECOND, path-sensitive pass (`insert_dec_on_dead_paths`, same
function): walks the body via `Dce.free_vars` and inserts an additional drop
exactly where the self-binding becomes dead, leaving live (recursive) paths
untouched. Both the unconditional early drop and the path-sensitive walk are
required — two intermediate versions were each broken a different way: the
walk alone left the protective inc permanently uncanceled on the recursive
path (rc grows unbounded); a naive "recurse into the let's tail" version of
the walk inserted a spurious SECOND drop on the live path when the consuming
call lived in the let's RHS rather than its tail (`let $t = ...call_ptr
helper(...) in +($t,k)`) — a double-free waiting to happen, not merely a
missed leak. Measured: `outer()` materializing a self-recursive `helper`
capturing `k`, looped 20,000 times, `LEAKED 20000` before, `BOUNDED` after.
Verified non-vacuous (via a `DUNE_CACHE=disabled` rebuild of the pre-fix file —
`--force` alone was insufficient, dune's shared cache served a stale artifact
on the first attempt). Two new regression tests in `test/test_codegen.ml`'s
`string_literal_codegen` group: the leak-bound assertion, and a fib-shaped
double-recursive-call correctness check (compiled vs. interpreted parity) to
catch an over-eager drop as a wrong value or crash rather than growth.
