Investigated as a related follow-up but NOT fixed at the time (since FIXED —
see "Third attempt" below): extending this same capture-free-under-REPL gap
(the note above) by threading an `is_repl` flag into
`insert_apply_fn_clo_drop` reintroduced the EXACT SIGSEGV the guard exists to
prevent (`repl_jit_cross_line` test 7, "stdlib List.length via precompile") —
reverted. `task_spawn`-ing a genuinely capture-free closure from a REPL
fragment still leaked one allocation per materialization.

**Second attempt (2026-07-30), one real bug fixed, the leak still open.**
Traced the crash to a genuine, independent gap: `lib/tir/llvm_repl.ml`'s
persistent REPL variable slots (`@march_repl_get`/`@march_repl_set`,
`runtime/march_extras.c`'s `march_repl_slots` array) did **zero RC
bookkeeping** — every read of a heap-typed slot (a String, a closure, any
boxed value) handed out the slot's own reference with no dup, and every
overwrite discarded whatever reference the slot held with no release. A
capture-free closure bound at the REPL prompt and read back from a later
fragment was freed on the first call as a direct consequence: the read gave
the caller the slot's only reference, that reference got consumed, and the
slot was left holding a dangling pointer for its next read.

Fixed at the LLVM-emission call sites — `emit_prev_slot_bridges` and
`emit_slot_loader_fns` (two independent read sites for the same slot; a
prior binding can be referenced either bridged directly into a later
fragment's entry block or via a generated zero-arg loader function) now
`march_incrc` a heap-typed slot's value on every read; `emit_store_to_slot`
`march_decrc`s whatever the slot held before a write replaces it (this
matters concretely for the `"v"` magic slot — the last-expression-value
binding, genuinely reused/overwritten across every subsequent REPL
expression, unlike an ordinary `let` which gets a fresh slot per
declaration). All three gated on the *static* `Tir.ty` at the LLVM-emission
call site, deliberately **not** inside `march_repl_get`/`march_repl_set`
themselves: slots store `Int`/`Bool`/`Float` **UNTAGGED** — unlike March's
usual `(n<<1)|1` tagged-immediate convention — so a blind
`IS_HEAP_PTR`-gated incrc/decrc inside the untyped C functions would have
misfired on an ordinary integer whose raw bit pattern happens to look
pointer-shaped (caught this before it shipped, via a `git show`-sourced
diff against the pre-edit file — never actually built or run). Verified:
all 24 `repl_jit_cross_line`/`repl_jit_regression` tests pass with this fix
alone, including "var redefinition" and "P0: List.length x3 successive
fragments" — the shapes that actually exercise repeated slot access.

Re-threading `is_repl` on top of this fix hit the **same** SIGSEGV, in the
**same** test, at the **same** address (`0x0`, frame `0x0` — a jump through
a null code pointer). So the slot mechanism was real but not the whole
story: there is at least one more path, somewhere in how the precompiled
stdlib passes a capture-free closure around internally, that is not yet
understood. Reverted the `is_repl` threading again (verified via
`grep is_repl lib/tir/perceus.ml lib/jit/repl_jit.ml` → no matches);
**kept** the slot RC fix, since it is a real, independent correctness fix
regardless of whether the capture-free leak is ever closed.

**Third attempt (2026-07-30) — FIXED, and the crash the first two attempts
kept hitting was never the mechanism either of them guessed at.**

Diagnosed from an actual stack trace this time (`lldb -b` on
`_build/default/test/run_codegen.exe -e repl_jit_cross_line`, with the drop
forced on via a temporary env-var hack), which neither prior attempt took:

```
frame #0: 0x0000000000000000
frame #1: repl_0.so`go$apply$218 + 432
frame #2: repl_0.so`List.length$List_Int + 68
```

The crashing closure is `go`, the inner **self-recursive** helper of
`List.length` — and it is capture-free, which is exactly why a blanket
"drop capture-free apply fns under REPL" hit it. The over-release is
straightforward once the frame is visible: a self-recursive capture-free
apply fn ALREADY releases its own reference, through `lift_lambda`'s
self-binding alias, via completely ordinary RC insertion. Confirmed in the
emitted TIR (`MARCH_DUMP_TXT=perceus`):

```
fn go$apply($clo, lst, acc) =
  let go = $clo in
  case lst of
    Nil()        -> dec_rc go; dec_rc lst; acc
    Cons($f,$t)  -> ... call_ptr go(t, $t2)
```

`dec_rc go` on the dead path, transfer into `call_ptr go(..)` on the live
one — the alias is an ordinary owned local and Perceus handles it correctly
with no help. Adding an unconditional entry drop on top is a SECOND release
of the same reference: rc hits 0 on the first call, the closure is freed,
and the recursive dispatch reads a zeroed apply-fn slot. Hence frame #0 =
0x0. Native never showed it because `static_closure_ok` routes native builds
to the immortal global, where the over-release cannot reach 0.

**The fix, and the third mechanism.** Two changes, each covering one of the
two capture-free shapes; the second was found by measurement AFTER the first
was green, and is genuinely independent:

1. `lib/tir/perceus.ml` `insert_apply_fn_clo_drop ~repl` (threaded through
   `insert_rc` ← `perceus ~repl` ← `lib/jit/repl_jit.ml`'s `lower_module`,
   which passes `~repl:true` to match `Llvm_emit`'s `ctx.repl` exactly).
   Under REPL only, an apply fn whose body mentions `$clo` **nowhere** gets
   an entry `dec_rc $clo`. The `not (mem $clo (free_vars body))` test is the
   whole correctness story: it is precisely what excludes the self-recursive
   case above, which mentions `$clo` via its self-binding and must stay
   untouched. Covers capture-free **lambdas**.

2. `lib/tir/llvm_calls.ml` `clo_wrap_define ~drop_clo` (passed
   `~drop_clo:ctx.repl` at all three emission sites — two in
   `lib/tir/llvm_emit.ml`'s `emit_atom`, one in `lib/tir/llvm_repl.ml`'s
   `emit_repl_fn_with_closure_slot`). Covers capture-free **top-level
   function values**, which materialize an `@<fn>$clo_wrap` trampoline that
   is synthesized at LLVM emission and has no TIR apply fn at all — so
   Perceus can never reach it, and change (1) alone left it leaking at the
   full pre-fix rate. This is sound alongside PR #123's slot-read `incrc`:
   a slot-held closure now hands out a fresh reference per read, which the
   wrapper's drop balances.

**Measured** (new `test/test_codegen.ml` regression,
`repl_jit_cross_line` "capture-free closure materialization does not leak",
reading `march_live_allocs` via `dlsym` out of the JIT's own runtime `.so`
so the fragments under test stay ordinary REPL input): over 2,000
materializations in a REPL fragment, both shapes grew by exactly **2,000**
before and **0** after. Each half is pinned independently — with only (1)
applied, the lambda phase reports 0 and the top-level-fn phase still
reports 2,000.

**Native impact: none, and proven rather than argued.** Both arms are gated
on `repl`/`ctx.repl`, and a program exercising both closure shapes plus
`List.length` emits **byte-identical** `--emit-llvm --opt 2` IR before and
after (928 lines, `diff` clean), with identical program output.

Verified: `repl_jit_cross_line` fully green (10/10 including the new test)
and re-run 20x for the flakiness this area has a history of (0 failures);
full `dune build --root . @runtest` green apart from
`adversarial-regressions` 39 (`MARCH_SANITIZE` 30s timeout), confirmed
**pre-existing** by reverting the five source files to base via file-copy
swap (never `git stash` in a march worktree) and reproducing it there.

  **Follow-up audit (2026-07-29, same day): the $clo drop is only sound because every C-runtime call site that invokes a closure's apply function now agrees on the calling convention — and four distinct families of call sites did not, each fixed in `runtime/march_runtime.c` / `lib/tir/llvm_emit.ml`:**
  - `__try_call`/`__try_call_val` (runtime/march_runtime.c) called their thunk once then explicitly `march_decrc`'d it — a second consumption of the reference the thunk's own apply function already released. Flaky, not deterministic (6/30 crashes on a single-capture thunk before the fix, 0/30 after) — freed memory frequently still looked valid enough not to crash immediately. `__try_call` is directly user-callable (`tir_names.ml` documents this) and one of `test/imports/erased_clo_native`'s dune-rule golden tests hit this exact shape, which is how it was traced after CI stayed red past the first (task_spawn) fix.
  - `march_typed_array_map`/`_fold` and `native_int_arr_map`/`map2`/`native_float_arr_map`/`map2` call the SAME closure once per array element without transferring ownership per call — fixed with `march_incrc` before each call plus one `march_decrc` after the loop, since the closure arrives as exactly one transferred reference (confirmed via TIR: `inc_rc closure; NativeArray.map_int(a1, closure)` is emitted only when the closure is still live afterward).
  - A parallel, LLVM-IR-level inline fast path for the same builtins (`lib/tir/llvm_emit.ml`'s `emit_native_map_inline_loop`/`emit_native_map2_inline_loop`, P10 Phase 2c) bypasses the C runtime entirely and needed the identical incrc-per-call-plus-final-decrc fix at the IR-emission level — this is why `test/native/native_arr_map_inline_capture.march` and 4 sibling goldens kept crashing even after the C-runtime functions were fixed.
  - `march_signal_drain` calls a registered watcher closure repeatedly, once per signal delivery, for the program's entire lifetime, without transferring ownership on any call — fixed with `march_incrc` before each `apply()` call and (unlike the array builtins) no matching decrc, since the watcher table's one held reference persists across drains and is released only by the existing replace/unwatch path. Deterministic, not flaky: a capturing watcher raised twice crashed on delivery 2 every run before the fix.
  - The actor message-dispatch loop (`runtime/march_runtime.c`'s `actor_green_thread`) has the identical shape — a long-lived closure invoked once per inbound message — and received the same defensive fix, though whether today's actor lowering can actually produce a CAPTURING dispatch closure (as opposed to always capture-free) was not independently confirmed; applied given the severity of the failure mode and the exact shape match with the confirmed Signal.watch bug.

  All four were caught by running `dune build @runtest` (the full CI-equivalent suite, including every dune-rule golden/native test) — `scripts/run-tests.sh` and the four alcotest binaries alone never exercise dune-rule tests and reported fully green while `test/imports/erased_clo_native` and 5 native array-map goldens were failing in CI. New alcotest regressions in `test/test_codegen.ml`'s `try_call_capture_ownership_codegen` group cover all four shapes (single-capture `__try_call`/`__try_call_val`, the panic/longjmp path, a closure reused after `NativeArray.map_int`, and a Signal.watch handler delivered 3 times), each run 15–30x since several of these are heap-timing-dependent rather than deterministic.

- ⏳ **(original report, retained)**  A closure that captures a free variable is allocated and never released, even when it never escapes. Minimal repro, compiled `--opt 2`:
  ```march
  fn go(i : Int, acc : Int, k : Int) : Int do
    if i <= 0 do acc
    else
      let f = fn x -> x * k
      go(i - 1, acc + f(i), k)
    end
  end
  ```
  At 4,000,000 iterations this reports `obj_allocs 4000000` under `MARCH_STRING_STATS=1` and peaks at **131.6 MB** RSS (measured 2026-07-28; `march_string_stats obj_allocs 4000000`, `maximum resident set size 131563520`, `24000006000000` for `go(4000000, 0, 3)`), against **2.9 MB / 0 allocs** for the identical loop with the capture inlined (`fn x -> x * 2`, which the optimizer eliminates entirely — `march_string_stats obj_allocs 0`, `maximum resident set size 3031040`). RSS grows linearly with the iteration count on a flat (tail-recursive) stack, so this is a leak, not churn. **Distinct from the capture-free case fixed by static closures:** a capturing closure's contents differ per instance, so it cannot be a module-lifetime static object — this needs a genuine ownership fix in `lib/tir/perceus.ml`/`lib/tir/borrow.ml`, not a codegen change. Note the same shape leaks whether the closure is called directly or passed to a higher-order function, so the call boundary is not the discriminator. Interpreted execution is unaffected (OCaml GC).

- ✅ **FIXED (2026-07-28): compiled-only SIGSEGV — a zero-arg top-level function materialized as a first-class value, then invoked through that value.** Minimal repro:
  ```march
  mod Main do
    fn answer() : Int do 42 end
    fn main() : () do
      let zf = answer
      println(int_to_string(zf()))
    end
  end
  ```
  Compiled `--opt 2`: exit 139 (SIGSEGV), no output (reproduced 2026-07-28). Interpreted: prints `42`, exit 0. Calling `answer()` directly (no `let` alias) works fine both compiled and interpreted — the crash is specific to materializing a *0-arity* named function as a value and then calling through the value; multi-arg named functions used the same way (`let af = add; af(7, 8)`) are unaffected. **Bisected to predate the static-closure work**: reproduces identically at `fbea848b` (the parent commit of the static-closure change) and at current HEAD, and independently corroborated by a diff read of the static-closure commit, which only touches the `Tir.TFn _` arm of the function-value materialization path in `lib/tir/llvm_emit.ml` and leaves the zero-arg fallback arm (the `| _ ->` "0-arg function / module-level constant" branch that calls the function to obtain its value) byte-identical. This is why `test/native/static_closure_semantics.march` (the Task 3 parity fixture) deliberately omits a zero-arg function-value case — including it would crash the build. Full bisection detail in `.superpowers/sdd/2026-07-28-static-capture-free-closures/task-3-report.md`.

  **Root cause:** typechecker gap, not codegen — codegen agrees with (and faithfully compiles) the typechecker's answer. March's zero-param T-Abs convention collapses a `fn`'s type to its bare return type (no `TArrow` wrapper), so `answer` referenced bare is genuinely typed `Int`; `--dump-tir` confirms `let zf : Int = answer`. `zf()` — calling a plain non-arrow-typed value — was then silently accepted by `infer_app`'s `| [], t -> t` base case (needed so a genuine zero-arg `fn()` call still works), which can't distinguish "callee is a disguised zero-arg fn" from "callee is a plain value" once no args remain to unify against. The exact same hole independently allowed `let x = 5; x()` to pass `--check` and crash — see `root_cap()`'s scope note (above, 2026-07-22) which first identified and deliberately left this open pending a safe general fix.

  **Fix (`lib/typecheck/typecheck.ml`):** generalized the `root_cap()`-only `noncallable_error` check in `Ast.EApp`'s handler to a positively-scoped signal rather than trying to negatively infer "not a known function" (which was the blocker noted in the `root_cap` scope note). New env field `plain_let_names : StringSet.t`, populated at exactly ONE site — the `Ast.ELet` case of `infer_block`, when the pattern is a simple `Ast.PatVar` and the binding is non-linear — marking that the name currently refers to a plain `let`-introduced value. `EApp`'s handler now rejects a zero-arg call `f()` when `f` is `EVar name` with `name ∈ env.plain_let_names` and its resolved type is a *concrete* non-arrow (excluding `TVar`/`TError`). `bind_var`/`bind_linear` clear the marker on any rebinding of the same name (mirrors `fn_arities`'s existing shadow discipline). Two exclusions were required to avoid false positives, both caught by the full suite before landing:
  - **`fn_arities`-absence was tried first and rejected as unsafe**: it's cleared by `bind_var` on ANY same-name rebinding (deliberate, for its own arity-check purpose), but a bulk `import Mod` rebinds every imported name via `bind_var` too — at multi-file scale (`test_large_multi_file_check_is_not_quadratic`, 2500 files importing one shared module), one importing module's `import Shared` wiped the global `fn_arities` entry for `helperN`, and EVERY later module's plain `helperN()` call misfired. `plain_let_names` is immune since it's only ever added to, never used as a negative/absence signal.
  - **A local zero-arg LAMBDA LITERAL bound via `let`** (`let g = fn -> body; g()` — real, tested behavior, `test/native/unit_callback_zero_arg.march`) collapses to its body's type under plain inference for the identical structural reason a top-level zero-arg `fn` does (`ELam`'s inference arm folds `TArrow` over zero params = no-op). Excluded by never marking `plain_let_names` when the `let`'s RHS is itself `Ast.ELam _` — a lambda literal is never a "disguised alias", so the AST shape (not the collapsed type) is the safe discriminator.
  - Local named functions (`fn helper() do ... end`, i.e. `Ast.ELetFn`) collapse to their return type the same way top-level `fn`s do; these are exempted by extending `env.fn_arities` registration (previously top-level-`fn`-only) to also cover `ELetFn` bindings, so `arity_error`'s existing direct-call machinery (unrelated to the new check, which only fires for `plain_let_names` members) keeps working for them, and aliasing one through a plain `let` (`let g = helper; g()`) is correctly still rejected.
  - Qualified cross-module calls (`Dep.bar()`) and bare cross-module builtin calls (`pmap_threshold()`, `self()`, etc.) are unaffected because they never flow through `Ast.ELet`, so they're never in `plain_let_names` to begin with — no denylist/allowlist needed for them at all (simpler than the `root_cap`-era design anticipated).

  **Tests** (`test/test_compiler.ml`, `typecheck` group): the pre-existing `test_tc_nonfunction_local_value_call_known_gap` (pinning the `let x = 5; x()` gap as NOT an error) was inverted to `test_tc_nonfunction_local_value_call_fixed` (now IS an error, message names `x` and says "not a function"); new `test_tc_zero_arg_fn_value_call_rejected` (the exact filed repro, `let zf = answer; zf()`); `test_tc_zero_arg_local_fn_still_callable` and `test_tc_zero_arg_local_fn_called_from_sibling_local_fn` (local `fn ... end` calls, direct and cross-sibling, still typecheck); `test_tc_zero_arg_local_fn_value_call_rejected` (aliasing a local fn through a plain `let` is rejected too); `test_tc_zero_arg_qualified_call_still_callable` (`Dep.bar()` unaffected); `test_tc_zero_arg_lambda_let_still_callable` (the lambda-literal exclusion, mirroring `unit_callback_zero_arg.march`). `test_srec_pingpong_loop_typechecks`'s fixture source was adjusted (`let ch2 = ...; let _ = ch2; ()` → `let _ = Chan.send(...); ()`) after the fix correctly exposed a pre-existing, unrelated parser glomming ambiguity (a bare local var immediately followed on the next line by a `()`-led expression parses as a call — documented in `specs/lang` parser notes) that had been silently masked by the exact bug this fix closes. Verified both backends: `zf()`/`x()` now reject at `--check` with a clear diagnostic naming the value and explaining it's not a function, instead of exit 139 at `--compile --opt 2`. Full suite green: compiler 611/611, eval 256/256, codegen 502/502 (LLVM IR validity gate clean), stdlib (quick) 780/780; `scripts/check-docs.sh` clean.

- ⏳ **Capture-free LAMBDA values (as opposed to top-level named functions) still allocate one closure per materialization (compiled) — OPEN, found 2026-07-28 in final whole-branch review of the static-closure work.** `static_closure_ok`/`intern_static_closure` only fire for a top-level `fn`-declared function materialized as a value; an anonymous lambda expression that captures nothing, passed as a value at its own creation site, is neither a top-level named function (the case this branch fixes) nor a capturing closure (the other open item above) — it falls between the two. Minimal repro, compiled `--opt 2`:
  ```march
  mod Main do
    fn apply_it(f : Int -> Int, n : Int) : Int do f(n) end

    fn go(i : Int, acc : Int) : Int do
      if i <= 0 do
        acc
      else
        go(i - 1, acc + apply_it(fn x -> x * 2, i))
      end
    end

    fn main() : () do
      println(go(4000000, 0))
    end
  end
  ```
  Measured 2026-07-28 (`MARCH_STRING_STATS=1`, `--opt 2`, 4,000,000 iterations): `march_string_stats obj_allocs 4000000`, peak RSS **131.5 MB** (`maximum resident set size 131547136`) — the same shape and magnitude as the pre-fix top-level-function-value leak (125.4 MB) and the still-open capturing-closure leak (131.6 MB) above. **Distinct from both existing entries:** it is not a top-level function reference (so `intern_static_closure` never sees it — no `fn_name` lookup applies to an anonymous lambda AST node) and it is capture-free (so, unlike the capturing-closure case, it is a legitimate candidate for the same static-global treatment this branch gave named functions — no ownership/perceus work should be needed, just extending the eligibility check to cover defunctionalized capture-free lambdas). Needs its own fix in `lib/tir/llvm_emit.ml`'s closure-materialization path (or `defun.ml`, if capture-free-ness needs to be visible before the lambda is defunctionalized into a synthetic top-level function).

- ✅ **Tuple projection forwarding (`lib/tir/cprop.ml`, extends P13, 2026-07-28).** `let t = (a, b) in t.$fv0` now folds to `a`, exactly like the existing record case (`let r = {x=a,y=b} in r.x → a`) — closing the gap the original SCC-inliner/single-use-inlining assessment flagged as still open. Tuple field lists are keyed by `Tir_names.fv_field i` (0-based), matching `lib/tir/lower.ml`'s existing tuple-destructure lowering (`let (a, b) = rhs` desugars to that exact `EField` shape), so ordinary `let`-pattern tuple destructuring benefits automatically with no lowering change. Field-name collision with closure-capture struct fields (`defun.ml`, also `$fvN`, 1-based) is impossible by construction — `field_env` is populated purely from a binding's own shape (`ETuple`/`ERecord`/`EUpdate`/alias), never from the field-name string, and a closure-capture struct is built via `EAlloc`, never `ETuple`. DCE removes the now-dead tuple binding in the same `Opt` fixed-point iteration with no changes to `dce.ml`/`opt.ml`. **Discovered, deliberately NOT fixed here (separate, pre-existing issue):** `lib/tir/perceus.ml`'s `dup_field_results` (escape-safety dup for a field returned bare in result position) matches only `Tir.TRecord`, never `Tir.TTuple` — orthogonal to this change, filed as a follow-up. 7 new tests (`cprop` group: `field_fold_tuple`, `field_fold_tuple_second_element`, `field_fold_tuple_alias`, `field_fold_tuple_non_literal_element`, `no_tuple_field_collision_with_closure_capture`, `tuple_field_result_escape_unchanged`, `tuple_dce_removes_allocation_from_llvm`) plus a native golden (`tuple_destructure_dce`). No benchmark under `bench/` exercises this pattern directly (confirmed by inspection); `list_ops`/`binary_trees` run clean as no-regression controls.
- ✅ **Locale-independent ASCII case conversion, ~30× faster (2026-07-27).** `march_string_to_lowercase`/`to_uppercase` called ctype.h's `tolower`/`toupper` per byte. Correctness: those are **locale-sensitive**, and March strings are UTF-8 — measured on macOS, `en_US.ISO8859-1` maps `0xC3` → `0xE3`, and `0xC3` leads every 2-byte UTF-8 sequence, so conversion could silently corrupt the encoding. March never calls `setlocale`, but any linked library or embedding app can, which makes string semantics hostage to global process state. Replaced with a branch-free unsigned-wrap ASCII mapping: identical output under C, en_US.UTF-8, en_US.ISO8859-1 and tr_TR.UTF-8, and **0.60s → 0.02s** on `bench/string_case`. Correctness checked exhaustively rather than sampled — an independent driver compares the new mapping against C-locale ctype for all 256 byte values including the `'A'-1`/`'Z'+1` boundaries. The 30× was verified not to be work disappearing: `allocs` (406) and `copy_bytes` (~401MB) are unchanged. **`bench/string_case`'s checksum cannot detect that failure mode** — it sums `byte_size`, invariant under case conversion — now noted in `specs/benchmarks.md`.

- ✅ **`stdlib/string.march` claimed an SSO that does not exist (2026-07-27).** The module header had stated since 2026-03-19 that "the runtime uses a fat-pointer representation with small-string optimisation (SSO): strings of 15 bytes or fewer are stored inline without a heap allocation." Never true — every string is a refcounted heap allocation with a 24-byte header. Actively misleading for anyone reasoning about allocation cost, and it contradicts the measured phase 1 profile. Corrected, and `grapheme_count`'s docs now state that it counts codepoints despite the name, with the concrete divergent cases (combining marks, ZWJ sequences).

- ❌ **String hashing: measured, NOT changed (2026-07-27).** `march_hash_string` is scalar FNV-1a, one byte at a time. A word-at-a-time alternative benchmarked ~2× faster on realistic key lengths (2–29 bytes) — but the absolute figures kill it: 5.5ns per hash today, 2.7ns saved. A workload would need tens of millions of string-keyed lookups for that to register. Worth noting the measurement gap that prevented a better answer: **no benchmark in the repo exercises string-keyed maps** — `bench/hash_map_bench` makes 73 string allocations against 22.8M object allocations, i.e. it is Int-keyed. If string-keyed map performance ever matters (JSON objects, HTTP headers), that benchmark should exist before the hash is revisited.

- ✅ **JS backend emitted a closure's apply slot as the `$clo` wrapper object when the allocation was `EReuse`/`EStackAlloc` (`lib/tir/js_emit.ml`, 2026-07-27).** Reported as "`Json.to_string` crashes on any `Object` value under `--target js`" with `TypeError: f._0 is not a function`, correct interpreted and compiled native. Neither the `Object`-specific nor the `json.march`-specific framing survived: the `Array` arm fails identically, and a JSON-free fixture (a lambda passed to a local higher-order helper from a match arm) reproduces it exactly. The JS closure layout is `{$: "$Clo_name", _0: apply_fn, _1: fv1, ...}` and post-Defun dispatch calls `f._0(f, args)`, so slot `_0` must hold the RAW function — but `emit_atom` deliberately rewrites a module-level function name to its `name$clo` wrapper, which is an *object* (`{_0: fn}`), so that bare function references work as first-class values. `EAlloc` compensated with an `is_clo` tag check that emitted slot 0 through `emit_atom_raw`; `EStackAlloc` and `EReuse` — the two rewrites escape-analysis and Perceus produce *from* `EAlloc` — each re-implemented the tagged-object emission without it. A closure allocated in a match arm whose scrutinee cell is dead is reuse-eligible, so Perceus rewrites it to `EReuse` and dispatch then called a record. `json.march`'s `Array`/`Object` arms are exactly that shape (match on `jv`, then allocate a lambda for the local `map_list`), which is why every non-scalar `to_string` died. **Diagnosis note:** the first hypothesis was `EStackAlloc` — plausible, since `known_call.ml:71` matches a closure in precisely that node — and it was WRONG; distinguishing it took emitting a separate marker comment from each of the three arms, at which point the output showed `EReuse` unambiguously. Guessing between two same-symptom candidates would have produced a fix that changed nothing. The three forms now share one `emit_tagged_alloc` helper, so the closure rule can no longer be applied to one and missed by the others. Pinned by `test/native/js_closure_reuse_apply_slot.{march,expected}`, covering the general shape plus `Json.to_string` over an array and an object — a dune-RULE test, so it needs `dune build @runtest` and is invisible to the four alcotest binaries. Verified red before the fix through the real harness, and the fixture now agrees across all three backends (interpreted, native `--opt 2`, JS).
- ✅ **Single-use private-function inlining (`lib/tir/single_use_inline.ml`, `lib/tir/inline.ml`, `lib/tir/dce.ml`, 2026-07-27).** A new `single-use-inline` TIR pass, scheduled in the Opt fixed-point loop right after ordinary pure-only inlining, relocates a private top-level function's body to its call site when it has exactly one direct, arity-correct reference anywhere in the module — even when the body is impure — since Perceus has already made ownership explicit by that point in the pipeline, so substitution just carries the existing `EIncRC`/`EDecRC`/`EFree`/`EReuse` operations and their order along unchanged. Reuses the existing 50-node size limit, DCE-root/address-taken/collision-dispatch/hot-code-reload exclusions, and recursive-SCC detection from the ordinary inliner. **Post-implementation review found three Critical soundness gaps, closed in the same fix wave:** (1) a caller-local binding could shadow and silently capture one of the callee's free names — `single_use_inline.ml`'s `try_inline_call` now checks the callee's free-name set against the caller's bound set and conservatively keeps the un-inlined call on any collision; (2) `Inline.alpha_rename` mutated ONE ambient rename table across sequence siblings, case arms, and the default arm, so a binder in one arm could leak into an unrelated sibling, and never bound a nested `ELetRec`'s own parameters at all — replaced with a persistent, properly lexically-scoped environment (`SMap`) threaded per-subexpression, and freshening now also avoids every existing free/global name in the body, not just the parameter list; (3) a bare unqualified call (`helper(...)`) and a fully-qualified call (`Module.helper(...)`) to the SAME function were counted as two separate references instead of one, under-counting call sites — reference/successor accounting and SCC construction now resolve bare names against qualified top-level names first (exact name, then extern, then a same-suffix qualified alias), and an AMBIGUOUS bare alias (two same-named functions in different modules) conservatively marks every candidate target non-direct rather than guessing. Also fixed: the native-LLVM Dune regression gate raced two rules writing the same undeclared `.ll` sibling and treated `grep`'s "no match" exit status 1 the same as a real grep error (status ≥2) — rules now copy the fixture onto distinct declared basenames per rule and the check script distinguishes missing/unreadable input, a real match, and a genuine grep failure. **Verification:** focused `single_use_inline` (29 cases, was 24), combined `inline` (10, includes 4 new lexical-scoping regressions), DCE, mutual-TCO, and reduction-check groups all pass; full `run_codegen` suite passes including the native LLVM verifier corpus. **Corpus measurement (93 non-JS top-level native fixtures at the measurement commit, now 94 after an unrelated rebase — see `specs/progress.md`'s 2026-07-27 entries for the count correction and the independent no-op check on the added fixture):** 180 removed call/definition occurrences across 53 affected fixtures, 159 of them touching explicit RC operations; no runtime speedup was measured, only fewer emitted LLVM definitions and calls. Full detail in `specs/optimizations.md` and `specs/progress.md`.
- ✅ **`Json.parse` allocated ~2 heap strings per INPUT BYTE; rewritten as a byte-index scanner, plus a new `string_byte_at` builtin (2026-07-27, `stdlib/json.march`, `runtime/march_runtime.{c,h,mjs}`, `lib/typecheck/typecheck.ml`, `lib/eval/eval.ml`, `lib/tir/{defun,borrow,llvm_builtins,js_emit}.ml`).** `parse` opened with `string_split(src, "")` — one heap string per input byte — then rebuilt every token by consing those single-byte strings and `string_join`-ing them, so cost scaled with the SIZE of the document rather than with the number of strings in it; `to_string`/`escape_str` did the same on the output side. On a 239-byte document containing ~20 distinct strings, compiled `--opt 2` over 20,000 iterations: **261 → 21 allocations per parse** (21 is the number of strings the document actually holds, the floor for a parser returning owned `String`s), 486 → 58 for a parse + serialize round trip, `hist_le7` share 90% → 31%, wall clock 0.67 → 0.14s parse-only and 1.10 → 0.24s round-trip (same-session interleaved A/B, 4 rounds, identical to 0.01s). **The enabling primitive was missing:** before `string_byte_at(s, i)` (byte value `0..255`, `-1` out of range, allocates nothing) March had NO way to inspect one character of a string without allocating — both `string_split(s, "")` and `string_slice(s, i, 1)` allocate a heap string per character — so "just don't split up front" does not work on its own, because the scanner still must inspect essentially every byte to find token boundaries. **Dead end worth recording:** the first rewrite walked `string_to_codepoints` (a `List(Int)`) alongside a byte offset, passed the full 825-test stdlib suite, and then failed to LINK when compiled — `string_to_codepoints` and `string_from_codepoint` are interpreter-only, with no entry in `llvm_builtins.ml`/`defun.ml`/`js_emit.ml`/the C runtime, and nothing rejects them at typecheck time. **A green stdlib suite does not establish that stdlib code compiles** (those tests run interpreted). The final version scans BYTES, which is safe because every JSON structural character is ASCII and every byte of a UTF-8 multibyte sequence is `>= 0x80` — and which avoids the codepoint version's real hazard, that `string_to_codepoints` emits a raw byte and advances ONE byte on a truncated multibyte sequence, silently desyncing a width-derived offset and slicing at the wrong boundary. Behaviour verified identical over a 48-case corpus (all escape forms, 2/3/4-byte UTF-8 in keys and values, nesting, whitespace, 16 error cases), old-vs-new and interpreted-vs-native, error text included; two deliberate improvements: a non-ASCII character in an error message prints as itself instead of mojibake, and a keyword mismatch names the keyword (`expected 'null'`) not the byte (`expected 'l'`). **Found and fixed in passing:** `march_string_slice` in `runtime/march_runtime.mjs` was `s.slice(start, len)`, treating arg 3 as an END index rather than a LENGTH, so every JS-backend slice with a non-zero start was wrong (`String.slice("abcdefgh", 5, 3)` → `""` on JS vs `"fgh"` elsewhere) — load-bearing here because the new parser slices at non-zero offsets constantly where the old one never called `string_slice` at all; the three checked-in `march_runtime.mjs` copies that were byte-identical to canonical were re-synced. **Three pre-existing bugs found and deliberately NOT fixed**, to keep this a pure performance change: a qualified constructor PATTERN whose bare name is shared by another stdlib ADT silently takes the wrong branch when COMPILED (`Ok(Json.Array(_))` and `Ok(Json.Null)` fall through to `_` compiled but match interpreted — `Array` is declared in both `json.march` and `msgpack.march`, `Null` in `dataframe`/`json`/`msgpack`, so the qualification is dropped and the pattern is compiled against another ADT's tag; it fails OPEN, with no error or warning, and reproduces identically pre-rewrite); `Json.to_string` crashes on any Object on the JS backend (`TypeError: f._0 is not a function` from the match-lambda passed to `map_list`; reproduces identically pre-change, and the JS *parse* path is verified correct), and the number scanner rejects a signed exponent like `1e-5` (preserved verbatim from the old `json_is_num_char`, flagged in a comment at `is_num_byte`).

- ✅ **`Toml.parse` allocated ~3.7 heap strings per INPUT BYTE (worse than `Json.parse` before its rewrite); rewritten as a byte-index scanner (2026-07-28, `stdlib/toml.march`, no runtime/compiler changes — reuses the `string_byte_at`/`string_index_of_from` primitives added for the JSON and prior string-perf work).** State went from `(chars : List(String), line, col)` — built once via `string_split(src, "")` and walked by consing/unconsing one-character strings, with every token (string bodies, bare keys, digit runs) accumulated via `acc ++ c`, an O(n²)-copying pattern for an n-byte token — to `(src, byte_index, line, col)`, inspected with `string_byte_at` and materialised with `string_slice`/`string_join`, following the `Json.parse` template from commit `556cf7e9`. On a 340-byte document (title/owner/database/servers sections, arrays, an inline table, nested tables) compiled `--opt 2`, 2,000 iterations: **allocs/byte 3.69 → 0.37** (2,506,057 → 250,044 string allocations; `obj_allocs` also fell 3,272,000 → 1,738,000 from less list/tuple churn in the character-list walk). Escaped/multiline string bodies use the same run-slicing scheme as `Json.scan_string` (segments collected only around actual escapes, one `string_slice` in the common no-escape case); bare keys and digit runs (with `_` separators dropped) are each a single slice or a short run-join; the previous digit-to-int conversion additionally re-split its own digit string char-by-char (`string_to_int_digits` via `string_split`) — replaced with a `string_byte_at` loop, so this fix also removes an O(n²) hot path the JSON rewrite didn't have. `line`/`col` state, needed for `TomlError` positions, is rebuilt in one pass (`reposition`) after a raw byte-index scan rather than threaded through every character step. Verified via the full `test_toml.march` describe/test suite (unchanged, all passing) plus a 33-case round-trip corpus (all escape forms, multiline basic/literal strings including embedded `"`/`'` runs that don't close the triple-quote, 2/3/4-byte UTF-8 in keys and values, dotted/quoted keys, nested tables, arrays, inline tables, array-of-tables, comments, and 7 error cases), diffed old-vs-new and interpreted-vs-native. `col` now counts BYTES rather than decoded characters, matching the JSON rewrite's precedent (unchecked by any existing test).

- ✅ **`Yaml.parse` allocated ~5.56 heap strings per INPUT BYTE (worse than `Toml.parse`'s pre-fix 3.69); rewritten as a byte-index scanner (2026-07-28, `stdlib/yaml.march`).** Same `string_split(src, "")` + `acc ++ c` char-list pattern as Json/Toml, converted to a `(src, byte_index, line, col)` cursor using `string_byte_at`/`string_slice`/`string_join`, following the same template. Byte-value dispatch uses `match byte do N -> ... _ -> ... end` rather than `if`/`else if` chains where practical, sidestepping the chained-`if` `end`-counting pitfall (a `match` needs exactly one `end` regardless of arm count) that dominated the Toml rewrite's iteration count. Measured compiled `--opt 2`, a 361-byte document, 2,000 iterations: **allocs/byte 5.56 → 1.02** (4,016,065 → 738,040 allocations, a 5.4x reduction); residual allocation is mostly `string_trim` (which always allocates, even with nothing to trim) plus `YMap`/`YSeq` object construction (not string allocs). Verified via the existing `test_yaml.march` suite (unchanged, all passing) plus a 20-case round-trip corpus (nested block/flow structures, escapes, comments, `---` separator, bool/null spellings, negatives/floats, multibyte UTF-8, malformed-input errors) — byte-for-byte identical output old vs. new.
- ✅ **`Xml.parse` allocated ~2.92 heap strings per INPUT BYTE; rewritten as a byte-index scanner (2026-07-28, `stdlib/xml.march`).** Same template applied to tag/attribute-name scanning, quoted attribute values, and text content. Comment/CDATA/PI bodies (no entity expansion needed) collapsed to a single delimiter scan + one slice each; text/attribute-value scanning (which needs `&amp;`/`&lt;`/`&gt;`/`&quot;`/`&apos;`/`&#NNN;` entity expansion) uses the same run-slicing scheme as `Json.scan_string`. The output-side `escape_xml_text`/`escape_xml_attr` serializers had the identical per-character pattern and were rewritten too. Measured compiled `--opt 2`, a 616-byte document, 2,000 iterations: **allocs/byte 2.92 → 0.10** (3,600,066 → 124,047 allocations, a ~29x reduction). Verified via the existing `test_xml.march` suite (unchanged, all passing) plus a 10-case round-trip corpus (nested tags, quoted attrs, entities, comments, CDATA, DOCTYPE+PI, deep `find`, multibyte UTF-8, `to_string`/`to_string_pretty`, 3 malformed-input error cases).
- ✅ **`Regex` compiler allocated ~5.82 heap strings per PATTERN BYTE, and the matcher ~1.2 per INPUT BYTE per match attempt; both rewritten as byte-index scanners (2026-07-28, `stdlib/regex.march`).** Different shape from the data-format parsers: `compile()` used `string_chars(pattern)` plus per-character `RAClass` range expansion (parser-shaped, same fix as Json/Toml); the matcher's `atom_matches` tested every position via a `char_at` helper doing `string_slice(s, pos, 1)` on every byte probed, including every backtracking retry (not char-at-a-time accumulation, but the same underlying "materialise a string where an int would do" mistake). Fixed by making `RALit`/`RAClass` carry `Int` byte codes instead of 1-char `String`s, and rewriting `parse_atom`/`parse_class_body`/`parse_items`/`compile`/`atom_matches` to use `string_byte_at`. Measured compiled `--opt 2`: compile phase (49-byte pattern, 20,000 iterations) **5.82 → ~0 allocs/pattern-byte** (285 → 4 total allocations across all iterations); match phase (`find_all`+`replace_all` with `\d+` against a 265-byte input, 5,000 iterations) **1.215 → 0.064 allocs/input-byte** (a ~19x reduction; residual allocation is the unavoidable `string_slice`/`string_join` materializing actual match substrings). Verified via the existing `test_regex.march` suite (18/18 passing) plus a 62-case round-trip corpus (literals, classes, escapes, anchors, quantifiers, all 5 public functions, edge cases including empty matches and boundary positions), interpreted and compiled.
- ✅ **`Uri.encode`/`Uri.decode`/`Uri.decode_query` allocated ~2.96 heap strings per byte of the component being encoded/decoded; rewritten as byte-index scanners (2026-07-28, `stdlib/uri.march`).** `Uri.parse`/`to_string`/`merge`/`merge_strict` were already fine — they route through `string_split_first`/`string_split`/`string_slice`, which allocate once per delimiter-bounded SEGMENT, not per byte. The per-byte cost was isolated to `encode` (walked `string_chars(s)`, materialising a 1-char string plus a list cons cell for every input byte, even for a fully-unreserved string) and `decode`/`decode_query`'s shared helper (same `string_chars` walk, plus `decode_query` pre-processing every value with a full `string_replace_all(t, "+", "%20")` pass before calling `decode` — a second full pass with its own allocation). `encode` now tracks a run of unreserved bytes and slices it out in one call, only breaking the run at a byte needing `%XX` escaping; `decode`/`decode_query` share one `decode_scan(s, len, i, start, acc, plus_is_space)` byte-index scanner using the same run-slicing idea as `Json.scan_string`, which also lets `decode_query` fold the `+`-means-space convention into the same pass instead of a separate pre-pass. Measured compiled `--opt 2`, a 157-byte URL exercised through every public function, 5,000 iterations: **allocs/byte 2.96 → 0.73** (2,320,024 → 575,021 allocations, a 4.0x reduction). Verified via the existing `test_stdlib_suite.ml` "uri stdlib" group (11/11 passing) plus a round-trip corpus covering full/partial URLs, percent-encoded multi-byte UTF-8 (`%C3%A9`, `%E6%97%A5%E6%9C%AC`), and `decode_query`/`encode_query` over repeated/empty-valued/`+`-encoded params. **Found and deliberately NOT fixed**, pre-existing and reproduces identically on the unmodified file: `Uri.parse`'s authority/path split treats a scheme-less relative reference with no leading `/` as having an authority (`Uri.parse("../d")` yields `host=".."`, `path="/d"` instead of the RFC 3986 path-noscheme reading), which makes `Uri.merge`'s dot-segment resolution over such references — including the doc comment's own example — misbehave; unreachable from `encode`/`decode`/`decode_query`.
- ✅ **`Csv` measured, NOT rewritten (2026-07-28).** `stdlib/csv.march`'s public API (`read_all`/`each_row`/`each_row_with_header`) is a thin March wrapper around C-runtime builtins (`csv_open`/`csv_next_row`/`csv_close`, `runtime/march_runtime.c`'s `csv_parse_row_fields`) that already reads byte-at-a-time via `fgetc` into a fixed stack buffer with zero per-byte heap allocation, materializing exactly one string per emitted field — there is no `string_split(s, "")`/`acc ++ c` accumulation anywhere in the March-level file. Measured compiled `--opt 2`, a 284-byte 5-row/5-column document (with a quoted field containing an embedded comma and an escaped `""` quote), 3,000 iterations: **0.106 allocs/byte** — already well under the ~0.5 threshold that flagged the other five parsers, comparable to where they land *after* their rewrites. No change made.

This closes out the pure-March data-format-parser sweep from `specs/2026-07-26-string-performance-profile.md`: `Json.parse`, `Toml.parse`, `Yaml.parse`, `Xml.parse`, `Regex`, and `Uri.encode`/`decode` all rewritten as byte-index scanners; `Csv` measured and found already efficient.
- ✅ **Interpolating a String no longer costs an atomic refcount pair per operand (`lib/tir/lower.ml`, 2026-07-27).** `"a${s}b"` desugars to `to_string(s)`, which Show-dispatches to `Show$String.show` — a synthesized IDENTITY (`fn x -> x`, emitted by `emit_builtin_impls`, because a string already is its own representation). That call was *emitted* and then inlined away in the post-Perceus opt loop; since Perceus runs FIRST, it saw a live owned-argument call and bracketed it with `inc_rc`/`dec_rc`, and the later inlining removed the call and stranded the pair around a bare alias. `string_concat3` borrows all three arguments (`lib/tir/borrow.ml`), so the extra reference bought nothing, and it was charged to every interpolated String operand in every March program. **Two fixes, both at the source rather than downstream:** lowering elides the `Show$String.show` call outright (so Perceus never sees a call), and `lower_to_atom_k` no longer binds a fresh temp when the lowered RHS is already a *variable* atom — `let v = EAtom (AVar x) in k (AVar v)` ≡ `k (AVar x)` in ANF, but the alias reads to Perceus as a second owned reference, so eliding only the call just moved the pair onto the alias. **The `AVar` restriction is load-bearing and was found by test, not by inspection:** the first attempt passed *any* `Tir.EAtom` through, which drops the binder's `ty_of_expr` ascription — a literal atom carries no type of its own, so `println(:hello)`/`show(:world)` fell back to the untyped path and printed `()` instead of `:hello` (caught by `iface_impl_mono_codegen` cases 17-18, compiled Show$Atom parity). Measured on arm64, `"w${a}x"` vs hand-written `"w" ++ a ++ "x"`, 3M iterations: **181/173ms → 137/133ms**, with the two now lowering to byte-identical TIR; `MARCH_STRING_STATS=1` histograms unchanged, confirming refcount traffic and not allocation. `bench/tree_transform` and `bench/list_ops` unchanged. Pinned by the `interp_string_operand_rc` TIR golden snapshot, **verified to FAIL on the unfixed compiler** at both the lower and perceus stages. **Two traps worth recording.** (1) *Benchmarks of this shape have a ~25% first-position warmup penalty*: in an A/B where both halves compile to identical IR, whichever ran first measured 173ms against the second's 137ms — so the original report's "interp 358ms vs chain 285ms" was itself partly warmup, and any before/after claim here must hold the benchmark slot fixed. (2) *Two previously-attempted fixes were reported as "did not work" on a mistaken premise* — that `to_string` was already gone before pre-Perceus simplify. `MARCH_DUMP_TXT` (added in this pass) shows `Show$String.show(a)` alive and well at the `tir-simplify-pre` checkpoint; the call survives all the way into Perceus. `--dump-tir` is post-Opt and therefore cannot distinguish a pass that CREATED a construct from one that merely preserved it.
- ✅ **`MARCH_DUMP_TXT=<stage>` prints pretty-printed TIR at any pipeline checkpoint (`bin/main.ml`, 2026-07-27).** `--dump-phases` serialized every `snap_tir` stage to JSON but there was no readable text view of the intermediate stages, so diagnosing "which pass introduced this?" meant reading `--dump-tir`, which only shows the post-Opt end of the pipeline. `MARCH_DUMP_TXT` prints `Pp.string_of_fn_def` for every function at each checkpoint whose label contains the given substring (`all` matches every stage) — e.g. `MARCH_DUMP_TXT=tir-simplify-pre`. This is what localized the interpolation refcount bug above.
- ✅ **Phase 2 tasks 4 and 5 CLOSED without building, on measurement (2026-07-27).** **Task 4 (size-class freelist): do not build.** `bench/run_string_xlang.sh` compares `string_small_churn` against four baselines chosen to separate allocator overhead from representation — C++ (`std::string`, has SSO) 238ms, C (raw malloc) 400ms, Rust (`String`, no SSO — March's exact representation) 553ms, March 709ms, Python (pymalloc) 1249ms, all agreeing on the same checksum. Rust bounds what a freelist can win at ~1.3×, because a freelist attacks precisely the allocator and refcount overhead separating the two; C++ is ~3× and beats raw malloc, because you cannot out-allocate not allocating. The freelist buys roughly a third, forecloses the rest, and composes with nothing. **Task 5 (container-returning `split`): not needed as specified.** March has no flat array of heap pointers to build on (`Array` is a March-level persistent trie, `NativeArray` is unboxed int/float only), so it needs a whole new RC-aware container — and `String.index_of_from` already delivers 3.7× (975ms → 267ms counting 150K fields) with effectively zero allocation for scan-shaped callers. Reopen only with a workload that demonstrably needs every field retained.

- ⏳ **Small-string optimization (planned, not started):** `specs/plans/2026-07-27-string-sso.md`. Store ≤7-byte strings inline in the value using March's existing tagged-immediate scheme — `IS_HEAP_PTR` already rejects sign-bit-set values, so `march_incrc`/`march_decrc` need **no changes**, and it is not a type-system change. Measured ceiling: ~53% of allocations in real web templating (`bench/iolist_template`), against 42% in the synthetic churn benchmark. Two other workloads measured higher and are misleading — the CSV split is synthetic with 3-byte fields, and JSON's 90% is an artifact of a parser allocating 2.03 strings per input byte. Standing caution recorded in the plan: an SSO makes allocation-heavy code look fast without fixing it.

- ✅ **Many-part string interpolation desugars to a single `string_join` call instead of a `++` chain (`lib/parser/parser.mly`, 2026-07-26).** `desugar_interp` folded interpolation parts (`"${a}${b}"`) into a left-deep chain of `++` (`prefix ++ to_string(e1) ++ s1 ++ ...`); each `++` (`march_string_concat`) allocates and copies the whole growing prefix again, so a k-part interpolation copied O(k²) total bytes. Past `interp_join_threshold` (3) segments it now builds a `Cons`/`Nil` list of the parts and emits one `string_join(parts, "")` call — `march_string_join` already does a proper two-pass O(n) join (sum lengths once, allocate once, copy once), so no runtime change was needed, and `string_join` is registered as a bare global builtin identically to `++` (typecheck/eval/codegen all key it by name with no module qualification). Short interpolations keep the `++` chain: materializing the list costs more than the extra copy at that size, measured on arm64 over 2M iterations with short segments at 231ms (`++`) vs 303ms (`string_join`) for 2 segments, 307 vs 356 for 3, and 589 vs 564 for 5 — crossover just under 5. Because both shapes now reach downstream passes, the two consumers that pattern-match the desugared interpolation had to learn the join shape: `try_collect_interp` (`lib/format/format.ml`), which reconstructs `"${...}"` source — without it the formatter emitted a literal `string_join([...], "")` call and the stdlib formatter-idempotency tests failed — and `decompose_concat` (`lib/desugar/desugar.ml`), which splits a `~H` sigil into per-part IOList segments. The `~H` path was the dangerous one: `html_interp_to_iolist` matches `to_string(e)` *per part* to decide what to wrap in `html_auto_escape`, so an unrecognized shape collapses the template into one opaque part and silently stops HTML-escaping interpolations — reproduced with `~H"<p>${evil}</p>"` emitting raw `<script>` tags, escaping restored by the `decompose_concat` fix. `test/test_eval.ml` now asserts both desugarings (`parse interp`, `parse interp many`). `specs/lang/surface-syntax.md`'s operator table gained a note that `++` itself is still O(n) per call — so `acc = acc ++ x` in a loop is still O(n²) — pointing at `IOList` (`stdlib/iolist.march`) for that case, matching the note `String.concat`'s doc already carried. **Not done in this pass** (deferred): a memchr-based prefiltering pass for `march_string_contains`/`index_of`/`split`/`split_first`/`replace`/`replace_all` (`runtime/march_runtime.c:2550-2760`), which currently do a naive byte-by-byte `memcmp` scan.
- ✅ **String performance phase 2, tasks 1-3 (2026-07-27).** Three landed against the phase 1 profile, each verified by same-session A/B (absolute timings on this machine are not comparable across runs). (1) **`String.index_of_from`** — offset-aware search, removing the O(n²) trap in tokenizing: without a start offset, finding the next separator means slicing off the tail and searching again, re-copying the remaining bytes every step. Two phase 1 benchmarks had to be written around its absence. Counting 150K fields in an 800KB buffer: `String.split` + `List.length` 975ms vs an `index_of_from` walk at 267ms — **3.7× with effectively zero allocation**. (2) **memchr-based substring search** — a shared two-stage helper (memchr finds candidate first bytes, memcmp confirms) routed through `index_of`, `index_of_from`, `contains`, `split`, `replace`, `replace_all`; `last_index_of` stays scalar as it scans backwards. `bench/string_scan` **809ms → 20.8ms (39×)**, `string_parallel_scan` 390 → 171ms, `string_split_large` 1108 → 928ms; unchanged where no search is involved. Absolute throughput went from ~0.5 GB/s to ~40 GB/s. `replace_all` additionally jumps between matches and bulk-copies each span rather than testing and copying byte by byte — which RAISES its reported `copy_bytes` because the old byte-at-a-time loop bypassed the copy counter entirely. (3) **Three-way concat folding** — desugar collapses `++` chains of 3+ in groups of three, `ceil((k-1)/2)` allocations instead of `k-1`: a 5-part chain went 400,004 → 200,004 allocations over 100K iterations, and `bench/string_small_churn` 888.9 → 710.5ms with copying down 145.7 → 112.6MB. Fixed arity rather than the variadic n-ary concat originally planned, because March builtin signatures are `Mono (TArrow ...)` and the only variable-length alternative is `string_join`, whose cons-list materialization measured at 59% of its cost. **The `~H` escaping bug from PR #90 was reintroduced and caught by test**: `ESigil` desugars its content before HTML lowering, so the chain arrived as one opaque `concat3` and `html_interp_to_iolist` stopped matching `to_string(e)` per part — silently disabling escaping, a failure that fails OPEN. `decompose_concat` now flattens `concat3` as well as `++`.

- ⏳ **String performance phase 2, remaining:** task 4 (size-class freelist) opens with a decision gate — the string-literal fix already cut `string_small_churn` from 24,000,004 to 10,000,009 allocations, so most of the traffic that target aimed at is gone and the criterion must be re-measured before any code is written. Task 5 (container-returning `split`) is a decision task: March has no flat array of heap pointers (`Array` is a March-level persistent trie, `NativeArray` is unboxed int/float only), so a zero-cons split needs a new RC-aware container — and `index_of_from` now delivers much of the same win for scan-shaped callers without one. See `specs/plans/2026-07-27-string-performance-phase2.md`.
- ✅ **Docs site: site-wide ⌘K search (Pagefind full-text + stdlib symbol lookup) (2026-07-27, `docs/_includes/search.html`, `scripts/build-search-index.sh`, `scripts/serve-docs.sh`, `.github/workflows/deploy-pages.yml`, four `docs/_layouts/*.html`).** march-lang.org had no search outside the generated API reference. Pagefind now runs as a post-build step over `_site/` — the only point at which the ~53 Jekyll pages and the 114 standalone HTML pages emitted by the external `march_doc` tool are the same kind of artifact — indexing 167 pages, shipping with the site, no runtime search service. Pages opt in via `data-pagefind-body`, which Pagefind treats as exclusive once any page has it, so the playground/Tetris/perihelion/graph pages need no exclusion rules; the stdlib pages get the attribute injected into the BUILD OUTPUT (not the committed files) so a regeneration can't undo it. The same modal also loads `march_doc`'s existing `search-index.json` (2038 symbols, ~57 KB gzipped, lazy) and shows a capped "Standard library" group linking to definition anchors (`Array.html#fn-push`) — symbol lookup previously worked only from inside the API reference. Symbols match on NAME only, exact or prefix, so prose queries return prose alone (verified: `how do supervisors restart children` → zero symbols). **Non-obvious bug found in review:** the full-screen scrim covered only a sliver of the page — not z-index, but the CSS containing-block rule: `.d-nav`/`.m-nav` set `backdrop-filter`, which makes an element a containing block for `position: fixed` DESCENDANTS, and the layouts render the include inside the nav. Measured 846×126 (nav box) vs 846×998 (viewport) after reparenting to `<body>`. **Guard:** the index script fails the build below 100 indexed pages — Pagefind exits 0 and still writes a `pagefind/` directory when it indexes nothing, so a dropped attribute would otherwise ship a dead search box on a green CI run. **Open follow-up:** the 114 API pages keep their own ⌘K, so the keystroke still means something narrower there; unifying needs a change in the separate `march_doc` repo plus regenerating all 114 files.

- ✅ **`DataFrame.eval_agg` O(n) boxing overhead on Min/Max/Std/Variance (2026-07-27).** Reported as "~40ms fixed overhead per call regardless of column size" on `Sum`; a from-scratch multi-size microbenchmark showed it's not fixed at all — it scales linearly with row count (100K rows: ~8ms/call, 500K rows: ~43ms/call), because `eval_agg`'s `Min`/`Max`/`Std`/`Variance`/`Median` branches called `float_list(df, col)` to materialize a boxed `List(Float)` from the column's `NativeArray` before folding over it with `Stats.*`, paying an O(n) allocation on top of the O(n) reduction. `Sum`/`Mean` had already been fixed in the same session (commit `f4301ac0`, landed just before this investigation) by routing through `native_float_arr_sum`/`native_int_arr_sum` directly; this extends the same idea to `Min`/`Max`/`Std`/`Variance`. **False start, load-bearing lesson:** a March-level recursive index loop calling `native_float_arr_get(arr, i)` per element looks allocation-free but isn't — `get`'s `Float`/`Int` return is boxed at the call site under the generic calling convention any ordinary March function uses, so it pays the *same* O(n) boxing cost as `float_list`, just with one box per element instead of one box-plus-cons-cell. Confirmed by benchmarking the index-loop version directly: no improvement over the original. **Real fix:** six new compiler builtins that each stay inside a single C-level reduction loop, mirroring `native_float_arr_sum`'s existing pattern exactly — `native_{float,int}_arr_min`, `native_{float,int}_arr_max`, and `native_{float,int}_arr_sumsq_dev(arr, mean)` (sum of squared deviations from a precomputed mean — the stable second pass of the standard two-pass variance algorithm; not bit-identical to `Stats`' single-pass Welford but equally free of catastrophic cancellation, and verified numerically matching `Stats.variance_safe`/`std_dev_safe` to full precision on a test vector). Touched all five sites a native-array builtin needs (`runtime/march_runtime.c`, `lib/tir/llvm_builtins.ml` declare_sig + PDeclare, `lib/tir/defun.ml` builtin_names, `lib/eval/eval.ml` interpreter case, `lib/typecheck/typecheck.ml` type signature). `DataFrame.col_native_min_max`/`col_native_moments` replace the old `float_list`-based `Min`/`Max`/`Std`/`Variance` branches; `Median` is untouched (needs a full sort, a separate and larger problem — `Stats.percentile`'s `List.nth` on a linked list is itself O(n) per call, independent of this boxing bug). Result: Min/Std at 500K rows went from ~33-42ms/call to ~0.5-0.6ms/call (60-80x), matching Sum/Mean's existing native-path speed. **Regression benchmark:** `bench/dataframe_bench.march`'s new `bench_agg_overhead` compares direct `NativeArray.sum_float` against `DataFrame.eval_agg(Sum)` over 500K rows and panics if the ratio blows up past 20x while eval_agg takes ≥5ms absolute — verified it actually fires by temporarily reintroducing the `float_list`-based `Sum` branch (41ms, panicked as expected) and confirmed silent on the fixed code. **Process footgun, twice:** editing this repo from inside a worktree, passing the MAIN REPO's absolute path to a file-editing tool (rather than the worktree's own path) edits the wrong checkout with no error — happened once for `stdlib/dataframe.march` and again for all five compiler-builtin sites, caught only because the compiled benchmark showed zero improvement despite the source apparently having the fix; recovered by diffing the two checkouts, copying the main-repo edits into the worktree, and `git checkout --` to restore the main repo. Also relearned the existing "stale `_build/default/stdlib`" and "CAS cache lives per-worktree, not in the main repo" gotchas along the way.

- ✅ **Compiled string literals no longer leak once per evaluation (`lib/tir/llvm_emit.ml`, `lib/tir/llvm_ctx.ml`, `runtime/march_runtime.c`, 2026-07-27).** A string literal carries NO refcount obligation in TIR — `perceus.ml`'s `EAtom (ALit _)` arm returns the expression untouched, exactly as it does for a global `ADefRef` — but codegen emitted `@march_string_lit`, a fresh `rc=1` malloc, on every evaluation of the site. Nothing owned that cell, so no pass ever emitted a matching decrement and each evaluation leaked one string. **Why it hid for so long:** the two neighbouring shapes are both clean, so the obvious probes are vacuous. A let-bound literal (`let s = "xyz"`) gets an ordinary Perceus dec on its binding; a literal passed straight to a non-allocating call (`String.byte_size("xyz")` in a loop) is hoisted out of the loop by the optimizer and evaluated twice in total. Only a literal evaluated repeatedly as a direct OPERAND reaches it — canonically of `++`, i.e. exactly how ordinary string building is written (`acc ++ ", "`, `s ++ "\n"`). Measured: `let s = buf ++ "xyz"` over 2M iterations — 4,000,003 allocations against 2,000,001 frees, peak RSS 64.2MB, growing linearly with the iteration count; the same loop with two variable operands used 2.9MB. **Fix:** one immortal string per literal SITE. `intern_string_site` emits a null-initialised `internal global` beside the bytes, and `@march_string_lit_static` fills it once and returns the same pointer forever (atomic CAS, so a cold cell reached from two threads still yields one shared pointer; `march_runtime_wasm.c` gets a plain single-threaded counterpart, where the same per-evaluation growth was pushing the bump arena forward). The cell is `global`, not `constant`, so a stray RC op can never fault. **Why sharing is safe:** March strings are immutable and the runtime never mutates a string cell in place (`march_string_concat` allocates a fresh cell and reads both operands; no runtime path tests `rc == 1` on a string). `MARCH_RC_IMMORTAL` (1 << 40) is far enough above any reachable count that no sequence of decrements reaches the free-on-zero paths, and it makes the FBIP `rc == 1` uniqueness test in `llvm_emit.ml` always false, so an immortal cell is never reused in place. `march_free` — which bypasses the count entirely, and is what Perceus emits for a dead LINEAR/AFFINE binding — now skips immortal cells; defensive rather than a known reproducer. Post-fix the original repro matches the variable-operand control exactly (2.9MB). Guarded by `test_string_literal_operand_no_leak_compiled` (`string_literal_codegen` group), which reads the runtime's own `march_live_allocs` gauge through an extern rather than sampling RSS, and compares the SAME literal site across two loop lengths so the assertion is on GROWTH — one immortal cell per site is the intended permanent cost. Verified non-vacuous by reverting just the codegen arm: `LEAKED 20001` before, `BOUNDED` after. Interpreted execution was never affected (OCaml GC). Benchmarks show no regression; `bench/iolist_template.march` peak RSS 52.6MB → 45.4MB. The `llvm_builtins_preamble_golden` blob gained the new declare.
- ✅ **`Bool` and `Float` refinements are enforced (`lib/refine/smt.ml`, `lib/refinecheck/refine_check.ml`, 2026-07-27).** Both parsed and type-checked while producing no verification condition at all; probed against the built compiler first and confirmed inert — `needTrue(false)` against `{Bool | _ == true}` and `sqrtish(0.0 -. 1.0)` against `{Float | _ >= 0.0}` each exited 0 with zero warnings, while an `Int` control of identical shape in the same file reported. **Bool** needed only the missing dispatch: `is_bool_base` beside `is_string_base`, wired through `refined_param_ty` / `refined_scope_ty` / `return_refine_sorted` / `return_refine_ext`, with the value reflected at `Smt.SBool`. **Float** is a new sort. **The encoding is Z3's bit-precise IEEE-754 FloatingPoint theory, NOT reals, and that is a correctness requirement rather than a preference:** the query `¬(x >= 0.0) ∧ ¬(x <= 0.0)` is **sat** over `Float64` (witness `NaN`, confirmed by asking z3 directly) and **unsat** over `Real` (trichotomy). The checker reports exactly when it proves a predicate can never hold, so a reals encoding would flag that ordinary contract on every argument — a demonstrated false-positive source, not a theoretical one. Verified empirically end-to-end: a predicate whose only witness is NaN is SKIPPED, not reported. Equality is `fp.eq`, not SMT-LIB `=`: `=` on `Float64` is bitwise, under which `{Float | _ != 0.0}` would accept `-0.0`, a divisor as bad as `+0.0`; under `fp.eq` a negative-zero argument IS reported (confirmed with a `0.0 *. (0.0 -. 1.0)` fixture). **Sort plumbing.** The `sort` marker channel previously meant "None = Int, `Some s` = a DECLARED sort name"; `$Bool`/`$Float` are a third shape (built-in SMT sorts), so every consumer that treats an unrecognised `Some s` as an ADT name now tests `is_scalar_sort` first — otherwise `(declare-const b $Bool)` names a sort nobody declared. `fn_sig` gained `param_scalar`; `reflect_scalar` gained a `~sort`; `check_post` gained a `~scalar_env` for a clause's declared-`Bool` parameters (`scope` carries only *refined* locals, so a bare `b : Bool` was invisible to it). **Containment, since adding a sort is what has poisoned the shared `z3 -in` channel before.** A `sort_conflict` guard abandons any VC declaring one symbol at two sorts; `float_wellsorted` drops any term still mixing a float with an Int after the `fp_rewrite` pass; and a new `formula_wellsorted` drops a term in Boolean position that is not a formula. That last one fixed a **pre-existing** malformed-VC source found during the sweep: a bare Bool guard (`if j do needTrue(k) else 0 end`) reflected `j` as an integer constant and asserted it, drawing `invalid assert command, term is not Boolean`. March spells float comparison with the ordinary operators, so `smt_of` cannot distinguish Int from Float and builds `Smt.Ge` for both; `fp_rewrite` runs over the finished term once the declarations are final and converts only comparisons whose BOTH operands are float-sorted. **Out of scope, and silent rather than guessed:** symbolic float arithmetic in a predicate (`_ +. 1.0 > 0.0` — modelling it needs rounding-mode surface), `Float` record fields and ADT payloads, special-value predicates (`is_nan`), and a literal with no exact plain-decimal SMT-LIB form. Literal-only float arithmetic IS folded (`0.0 -. 1.0`), which is how the negative literal March has no direct spelling for is recognised. **9 new tests** (236 → 245) in `bool-refinements` and `float-refinements` groups, 5 of them asserting SILENCE. Adversarial sweep — unknown Bool/Float in the newly-checked position, a fact retired by each of the five binding forms, Float alongside Int/String/record/ADT parameters in one function, unproven Bool and Float postconditions, guards about the wrong value — all exit 0 cold-cache with **zero** `(error` lines on a tee'd solver channel, and a 12-violation-kind file reports all 12, also with zero errors. `test_refine`, `run_compiler`, `run_eval` and `scripts/check-docs.sh` clean. Docs: `specs/lang/refinement-types.md` (new Bool and Float sections plus rewritten limitations), `specs/lang/surface-syntax.md`, `docs/refinement-types.md`, `CHANGELOG.md`, `specs/progress.md`.

- ✅ **CAS key now digests the runtime directory that is actually compiled (`lib/cas/cas.ml`, `bin/main.ml`, 2026-07-27).** `Cas.runtime_identity` resolved its own runtime-directory candidates **cwd-first** while `bin/main.ml`'s `find_runtime_file` resolves the sources handed to clang **exe-relative first** ("independent of CWD", per the 2026-07-26 fix). From the repo root against `_build/default/bin/main.exe` those are different directories — and a targeted `dune build bin/main.exe` does not restage `_build/default/runtime` — so the key digested the edited `./runtime/*.c` while the compile used the staged copy: adding instrumentation to `runtime/march_runtime.c` printed `compiled <out> (cached)` for a binary with none of the new code. `compiler_identity` does not cover it (only the exe's bytes, unchanged by a runtime C edit); that claim in the cas.ml and runtime_archive.ml comments was wrong and is corrected. **Fix:** the driver resolves the runtime directory once (`runtime_dir`, `MARCH_RUNTIME_DIR` overriding, mirroring `MARCH_STDLIB`) and registers it via the new `Cas.set_runtime_dir` before any `compilation_hash`; `runtime_identity` became a memo keyed on the resolved directory instead of a `Lazy.t`, and its unregistered fallback now mirrors bin/main.ml's order exactly so the two cannot drift apart again. **Regression:** `test/cas_runtime_dir_check.sh` + `dune build @test/cas-runtime-dir` compiles one program against two divergent runtime trees through the same warm CAS store (asserting the repeat compile IS a hit, so the test cannot pass vacuously); confirmed RED with `set_runtime_dir` stubbed out. Plus two `test_cas.ml` unit cases. Also fixed `CLAUDE.md`, which named the artifact directory `artifacts/` — artifacts have lived in `artifacts-v2/` since the v1 pointer-store fix, so the documented clear command cleared nothing.
- ✅ **String performance phase 1: the measurement apparatus (2026-07-27).** Built to decide March's string representation question with data instead of asymptotic reasoning. `MARCH_STRING_STATS=1` counters in `runtime/march_runtime.c` (allocation count/bytes, size histogram at 7/15/23/31/63/255, bytes copied, frees, peak live bytes, non-string `march_alloc` counts), hooked into `march_string_alloc` and both `march_decrc` free paths, off by default behind a lazy env check shaped like `gc_trace_on`, verified at −0.34% overhead when off. Six benchmarks in `bench/` each isolating one cost, documented in `specs/benchmarks.md`, plus `bench/run_string_bench.sh` with an asserted-checksum gate (proved to fire), per-platform RSS normalization, and a `--verify-overhead` mode. Verdicts in `specs/2026-07-26-string-performance-profile.md` against criteria fixed before measuring: **SSO indicated** (91.7% of 24M allocations ≤23 bytes; doubling allocations doubles wall time), **array-returning split indicated** (1.82× slower than slice-walk while copying 1.43× the bytes — the difference is 9,000,120 cons cells vs 0), **views NOT indicated** (copy volume 0.55–0.79× of input, not the ≥2× required; each field is copied exactly once), SIMD search worth doing at ~0.5 GB/s but not the top win, **phase 3 gate triggered** (parallel scan plateaus at 3.97× on 8 of 10 performance cores, unexplained by cores or bandwidth). Three implementation corrections the measurements forced: an `obj_allocs` counter (cons cells are not strings, so the split/slice pair could not attribute list cost without it), byte-loop copy accounting for `to_lowercase`/`to_uppercase`/`reverse` (they transform while copying and never call `memcpy`, so `string_case` reported 1MB copied for 400MB of work), and O(n) reformulations of two benchmarks whose planned `index_of`-then-slice-the-tail shape was O(n²) (`String.index_of` has no start-offset variant).

- ✅ **Both phase 1 RC leaks are FIXED on main (2026-07-27).** Found by the phase 1 string harness, fixed independently: the string-literal-per-evaluation leak (immortal cell per literal site, `lib/tir/llvm_emit.ml`) and the container-released-without-destructuring leak (`fix(codegen): deep drop`, `bb069c88`). The phase 1 peak-RSS and peak-live columns were contaminated by these and must be re-measured against the fixed compiler before any memory-based criterion is applied.

- ⏳ **String performance phase 2 (pending):** fix the two RC leaks, then small-string optimization, then array-returning `split`, then `memchr`/SIMD search — in that order, per the phase 1 recommendation. Re-run the memory criteria after the leak fixes.

- ⏳ **String performance phase 3 (gated):** data-parallel chunked string operations. Blocked on explaining the 4-worker scaling ceiling (shared-owner refcount traffic vs allocator contention) before any chunking algorithm is written.

- ✅ **Refinement Tier 2: structural induction over recursive functions (`lib/refinecheck/refine_check.ml`, 2026-07-26).** A relational postcondition on a structurally recursive function — `fn insert(t : Tree, x : Int) : {Tree | size(_) == size(t) + 1}` — is now PROVEN at its definition and therefore propagates. Probed against the built compiler beforehand and confirmed skipped, not proven: the definition exited 0 (ambiguous under definite-failure) but `needs_empty(insert(Leaf, 5))` where `needs_empty(t : {Tree | size(t) < 1})` ALSO exited 0, so nothing travelled. Under definite-failure only a PROPAGATION test distinguishes proved from skipped, and every "now proven" claim below is backed by one. **Mechanism.** Z3 does no induction, but full induction is not needed: `check_post_induction` mints a fresh opaque constant for each self-recursive call, assumes the function's own postcondition about that constant *iff* the argument at the MATCHED parameter's position is a variable certified by `structural_subvars`, asserts the arm's pattern equation (`t = Node(l, v, r)` — without it even the BASE case is unprovable, since `size(t)` is otherwise arbitrary), and discharges each `match` arm separately against the `@[measure]` recursion equations. The induction is on the matched parameter alone, so only that argument must shrink and the hypothesis is universally quantified over the rest — a growing accumulator is fine. **Soundness.** The gate is `structural_subvars`, unwidened — the same one that makes `@[measure]` axiomatisation sound. A proven postcondition is ADDED to the assumption set call-site checks prove `¬goal` against, and adding assumptions makes a violation EASIER to prove, so an unsound hypothesis manufactures FALSE POSITIVES on correct code rather than merely failing to help; the non-structural case is pinned by a test asserting silence on a program that would be reported under a looser gate. **Containment.** The path occupies the previously-inert variant-ADT-return case (`return_refine_ext` returns `None` for it, so nothing happened there at all), and is VERDICT-ONLY — it never emits a diagnostic, so `check_post` remains the single reporting site and a Tier 2 definition is silent whatever the solver says. Its only observable effect is enabling propagation through `gate_unverified_posts`. `fn_sig` gained `ret_sort`, so an ADT-sorted postcondition is carried only by `reflect_dt` (which knows the datatype sort) and never by the `Int` consumers `reflect_scalar`/`scope_add_binding`, which would declare one symbol at two sorts — the z3 `(error …)` that desynchronises the shared solver channel. A per-VC `declare` guard abandons any VC that would do so anyway. `Int`/record postconditions go down the unchanged Tier 0/1 path. `--no-measure-axioms` disables the path (it empties `measure_preamble_sorts`, which the VCs require), pinned by a test. **16 new tests** in a `tier2-induction` group (184 → 200), 12 of them asserting SILENCE. Newly proven and propagating: the canonical tree `insert`; a user `@[measure]` over the built-in `List`; recursion into the SECOND recursive component; a closed (non-relational) ADT postcondition. Pinned as silent with the reason recorded at each: the built-in `len` (not an axiomatised measure, so no recursion equations), mutual recursion (the hypothesis is minted only for the function's own name), a recursive call inside a lambda or behind a nested `match` (only the top-level pattern equation is built), a true-but-unprovable postcondition, a recursive function with no postcondition, and a closed `Int` postcondition. Adversarial sweep (4 programs — a true-but-unprovable postcondition, lambda- and nested-match-hidden recursion, a growing accumulator, and a module combining Tier 2 with records, strings, ADT testers, callbacks and Tier 1 relational propagation) all exit 0 cold-cache; `test_refine`, `run_compiler`, `run_eval`, `scripts/check-docs.sh` and ten stdlib-using example/bench programs all clean. **HAMT frontier.** A local reduced model of `stdlib/map.march` (the stdlib was not modified) shows THREE stacked obstacles: (1) the built-in `List` is modelled with an OPAQUE element sort (`Elem`), so a count measure that must recurse into a list element is ill-sorted and never axiomatised at all — swapping in a user-defined child list, changing nothing else, makes the same postcondition prove and propagate, which pins this as the first blocker, *before* induction is even reached; (2) with that worked around, `node_insert` reaches its child through a NESTED match whose pattern equation is not built; (3) the real code fetches the child by INDEX (`list_nth_safe(children, idx)`), which `structural_subvars` cannot certify and which would need a length/termination lemma — on top of `HEntry` being generic, wrapped in a `ptype`, and carrying an `eq` callback and a `level` counter. So the plan's question "does the structural test recognise a child fetched from a `List` by index?" is answered **no**, and obstacle (1) precedes it. Docs: `specs/lang/refinement-types.md` (Limitations rewritten — the "needs induction the solver can't do" framing was partly wrong — plus the HAMT frontier and an updated conformance count), `CHANGELOG.md`, `specs/progress.md`.

- ✅ **Higher-order refinement checking: calls through a refined callback parameter and through a local alias (`lib/refinecheck/refine_check.ml`, 2026-07-26).** Two call shapes `visit`'s `EApp` case previously skipped entirely, since `resolve_call` only resolves a NAMED callee: a call made *through* a refined function-typed parameter (`fn ap(f : ({Int | _ >= 0}) -> Int) : Int do f(-3) end`), and a call through a local alias of a named function (`let g = takepos  g(-3)`).
